al., 2023). They often host live question and answer sessions, conduct giveaways, or
share personal experiences with branded products, which not only entertain but also
educate the audience. Such engagement strategies create a lively forum for followers
to interact,  ask questions, and provide real -time feedback, deepening the bond
between the streamer and their audience (Jones & Lee, 2022).  For example, the
Chinese streamer Li Jiaqi, known as the “Lipstick King,” frequently engages his
followers by testing dozens of beauty products during live streaming, encouraging
viewers to comment on colours and textures in real time before making purchas e
decisions. Similarly, on Western platforms, TikTok Shop creators like Mikayla
Nogueira use candid product demonstrations and instant audience responses to foster
authenticity and trust. These real -world practices exemplify how interactive
communication and personal storytelling can transform passive viewers into actively
engaged consumers.

The impact of streamers on brand perception and the follower relationship is profound.
Their portrayal of the brand could significantly sway followers’ impressions, with their
credibility being a powerful tool in shaping brand image (Ku, Kao & Qin, 2019; Liu,

61

Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Wang et al., 2022) . When followers witness their favourite
streamers using or endorsing a product, the streamer's trustworthiness is transferred
to the brand, often leading to increased consumer trust and loyalty (Kim & Park, 2023).

Furthermore, the relationship between streamers and followers, nurtured by shared
experiences and interactions around branded content, could evolve into a strong
community. This community, centred around the streamer's platform, becomes a
fertile ground for brand growth. Streamers, by fostering this relationship, create an
environment where followers feel a sense of belonging, which could lead to a stronger
association with the brand and even brand advocacy (Ooi et al., 2023; Leung, Gu &
Palmatier, 2022; Teresa Borges-Tiago, Santiago & Tiago, 2023).
Streamers, therefore, are useful in bridging the gap between brands and followers.
Their influence extends beyond mere marketing metrics; they cultivate relationships
(Yuan & Lou, 2020; Caruelle, 2023) , shape perceptions (Wei et al., 2022; Yan et al.,
2022; Zhou & Lou, 2023) , and create online communities (Farivar & Wang, 2022;
Leung, Gu & Palmatier, 2022; Hsu, 2022)  that are necessary to the fabric of live
streaming commerce.

2.1.4.3 The Role of the Follower in Shaping the Interactions Between Brand and
Streamer
In live streaming, followers play a crucial role in shaping the relationship between the
brand and the streamer by influencing how interactions unfold and how marketing
messages are received. The brand –streamer relationship is not one -directional; it
evolves dynamically based on follower responses and participation during live
sessions. Followers act as both the audience and active co -creators of the live

62

streaming experience, influencing the authenticity and effectiveness of the brand –
streamer collaboration. Follower engagement with content is not just a passive
reception but a trigger for direct interactions with brands, demonstrated through likes,
shares, comments, and more resource -intensive actions like website visits and
product purchases (Hughes, Swaminathan & Brooks, 2019; Cabeza -Ramírez et al.,
2022; Fan et al., 2023) . This engagement, whether spontaneous or prompted by the
streamer’s cues, can enhance or diminish the perceived credibility of both the streamer
and the brand (Jin & Ryu, 2020; Lou, Tan & Chen, 2019; Dou et al., 2023).

Also follower engagement is especially potent during live stream shopping events,
where real -time interactions between followers, streamers, and brands flourish,
facilitated by platform features that enable instant purchases (Cabeza-Ramírez et al.,
2022; Caruelle, 2023; Ooi et al., 2023). For instance, when followers actively question
product quality or share personal experiences in real time, they indirectly validate—or
challenge—the brand’s claims, shaping the trust dynamic that underpins the brand –
streamer partnership.

The depth of followers' engagement with brand-related content is closely linked to the
strength of their parasocial relationship with is streamers. Stronger relationships could
lead to more engagement, though this effect can diminish if followers perceive content
sharing as primarily driven by profit or self -promotion rather than genuine
recommendation (Yuan & Lou, 2020; Bu, Parkinson & Thaichon, 2022; Caruelle,
2023). Followers also engage in brand conversations and electronic word-of-mouth by
commenting on live streaming content, which could lead to purchasing behaviours
(Giertz et al., 2021; Zheng, Li & Na, 2022; Caruelle, 2023; Ko, 2023) . Streamers

63

encourage this by promoting content creation among followers, who often imitate
streamers' brand endorsements without compensation, increasing brand visibility
(Sánchez-Fernández & Jiménez-Castillo, 2021; Wei et al., 2022; Caruelle, 2023).
Overall, followers are not just passive audiences but active agents who mediate and
reinforce the relationship between brands and streamers. Their engagement functions
as a critical feedback mechanism that influences how both parties adapt their
strategies in real time. For streamers, follower reactions —expressed through
comments, likes, and live interactions—serve as indicators of what content resonates,
directly shaping their communication style and promotional approach (Jun & Yi, 2020;
Caruelle, 2023) . For brands, follower engagement provides insight into audience
sentiment and brand perception, acting as a barometer of the effectiveness of the
partnership. When followers respond positively, they validate the authenticity of both
the streamer and the b rand, strengthening their mutual credibility (Hughes,
Swaminathan & Brooks, 2019; Wies, Bleier & Edeling, 2022; Zheng, Li & Na, 2022) .
Conversely, disengagement or negative feedback may prompt brands to reassess
collaborations or adjust their messaging strategies.

In this way, followers act as co -creators in the digital marketing—amplifying brand
messages, influencing streamer content, and shaping how brand –streamer
collaborations evolve over time (Delbaere, Michael & Phillips, 2021; Farivar & Wang,
2022; Leung, Gu & Palmatier, 2022; Wies, Bleier & Edeling, 2022) . Their active
participation thus transforms the brand –streamer relationship from a transactional
partnership into a dynamic, audience -driven interaction grounded in responsiveness
and shared engagement.

64

2.2 Theoretical Foundation
To understand the complex psychological and behavioural mechanisms in live
streaming commerce, this study adopts a multi -theoretical approach. Given the real -
time, interactive nature of live streaming commerce, where streamers, brands, and
followers constantly influence one another, a single theory is insufficient to fully explain
follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour. Therefore, this research draws
on Source Credibility The ory, Congruity Theory, Flow Theory, and Consumer
Engagement Theory. These theories complement each other by addressing different
aspects of the live streaming experience: how followers perceive streamers
(credibility), how value alignment shapes trust (con gruence), how immersive
experiences affect emotional connection (flow), and how all these elements contribute
to engagement (engagement theory) and impulse buying. Together, these foundations
support the development of a comprehensive conceptual model and justify the
proposed research hypotheses.

2.2.1 S-O-R framework
The Stimulus –Organism–Response (S -O-R) framework, originally proposed by
Mehrabian and Russell (1974), explains how external environmental stimuli influence
individuals’ internal cognitive and emotional states, which subsequently shape
behavioural response s. The  systematic review shows  framework has been widely
applied in consumer behaviour, digital marketing, and online shopping research to
explain how environmental cues affect consumer attitudes and actions (Zhang et al.,
2025). In the context of live streaming commerce, streamer attributes can be viewed
as stimuli, sense of presence, congruence, and dynamic brand experience represent
organism-related psychological mechanisms, while follower engagement and impulse

65

buying constitute behavioural responses. Therefore, the S -O-R framework provides
the overarching theoretical structure underpinning the conceptual model of this study.

2.2.2 Source Credibility Theory
Source Credibility Theory has long been recognised in marketing and communication
research as a foundational explanation of persuasive effectiveness. The theory posits
that the extent to which a message source is perceived as credible —specifically in
terms of expertise, trustworthiness, and attractiveness —directly influences how the
audience receives, accepts, and acts on the message (Hovland & Weiss, 1951; Gao
et al., 2021) (see Appendix 6). In the context of live streaming, where communication
is spontaneous, unfiltered, and interactive, the credibility of the streamer becomes
especially important. Unlike traditional media, followers in live streams are constantly
evaluating the streamer’s appearance, communication style, and authenticity in real
time, making these attributes a critical part of the consumer decision -making process
(Yang, Feng & Wang, 2019; Wang et al., 2021; Wellman, 2023).

Within this framework, trustworthiness refers to the follower’s perception of the
streamer as honest and unbiased, while expertise relates to the streamer’s perceived
knowledge, skills, or competence on the subject or product being promoted.
Attractiveness, meanwhile, extends beyond physical appearance to include likability,
relatability, and interpersonal appeal, which can foster stronger parasocial
relationships between the streamer and the audience (Gao et al., 2021). These three
dimensions interact to shape not just message acceptance but also emotional
connection and behavioural intentions.  Recent studies have also emphasised the
importance of authenticity—a sense that the streamer is genuine and consistent —as

66

a key extension of the traditional source credibility model, especially in digital and
streamers marketing contexts  (Halder, Pradhan & Roy Chaudhuri, 2021) . In live
streaming, where viewers expect transparency and spontaneity, authenticity acts as a
powerful enhancer of both trust and expertise  (Liu & Sun, 2023) . In this study,
streamers are viewed as interactive communicators, and their credibility is expected
to play a significant role in shaping followers’ perceptions, attitudes, and behaviours
toward both the streamer and the brand. Source Credibility Theory is  therefore used
to explain how streamer attributes serve as stimuli that initiate the perceptual and
emotional processes underpinning engagement and impulse buying in live streaming
commerce (Yan et al., 2022).

2.2.3 Congruity Theory
Beyond source credibility, the perceived “fit” or alignment between the brand, the
streamer, and the follower plays a crucial role in shaping persuasive outcomes.
Congruity Theory, originally proposed by Osgood & Tannenbaum (1955) , explains
how individuals evaluate and adjust their attitudes based on the consistency  or
inconsistency — between associated elements. In marketing the theory suggests that
a high level of congruence between a communicator’s image and the message or
brand they promote reinforces positive attitudes, whereas incongruence creates
cognitive discomfort and may lead to resistance or disengagement (see Appendix 6).
This concept has been widely applied in studies of endorser -brand fit, showing that
when an streamer’s personality, values, or lifestyle align with the brand’s identity,
audiences are more likely to trust the endorsement and respond favourably  (Liu,
Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Chen, Pham & Tran, 2024; Gong, Yang & Wu, 2024; Tian &

67

Frank, 2024). In the context of live streaming, congruity theory becomes even more
relevant. Followers often develop parasocial relationships with streamers and may
identify closely with their lifestyle or persona. Therefore, congruence between the
streamer’s personal brand, the endorsed product, and the follower’s self-concept can
heighten engagement and brand acceptance (Liu, Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Yang, Yang
& Men, 2023; Tian & Frank, 2024; Wang, 2024).
For instance, when a fitness streamer promotes a sports nutrition brand that aligns
with their lifestyle, followers experience less cognitive dissonance and are more likely
to perceive the endorsement as authentic and persuasive. In contrast, if the same
streamer were to promote a luxury watch brand with no clear connection to their
persona, the inconsistency might reduce trust and engagement.
Although Congruity Theory has been widely examined in traditional and digital
marketing, there is limited research on its application in live streaming commerce,
where engagement unfolds in real time and impulse decisions are common  (Hossain
et al., 2023; Yang, Yang & Men, 2023; Tian & Frank, 2024) . In such dynamic
environments, brand –streamer–follower congruence may not only shape initial
perceptions but also impact real -time behaviour, such as impulse buying or sharing
the stream. This study extends the application of Congruity Theory to live stre aming
contexts by examining how perceived fit influences dynamic brand experiences and ,
in turn, affects follower engagement and impulse purchasing. By addressing this gap,
the research contributes to a deeper understanding of how congruence functions in
fast-paced, interactive digital environments.

68

2.2.4 Flow Theory
To further explain how dynamic brand experiences are constructed during live
streaming, this study draws on Flow Theory ( Csikszentmihalyi,1995) and the related
concept of sense of presence (Perse & Courtright,1993; Draper et al., 1998), also it is
also essential for understanding how dynamic brand experiences are constructed
during live interactions.  Flow Theory describes a psychological state of deep
immersion in which individuals become fully absorbed in an activity, experiencing
intense focus, enjoyment, and a loss of awareness of time (see Appendix 6). In digital
marketing and media contexts, flow has been widely used to understand how users
engage with online content  as well as brand,  and how this optimal experience
enhances emotional involvement, decision-making, and consumer behaviour (Liao et
al., 2022; Li et al., 2023; Zheng et al., 2023; Tian & Frank, 2024).

Flow theory further supports the idea that such immersive experiences can lead to
deeper cognitive and emotional engagement. Especially in live streaming commerce,
presence plays a central role in achieving flow. Presence refers to the psychological
state in which individuals feel immersed and connected within a mediated environment
(Ma, Zou & Lv, 2022; You, Wang & Shamu, 2023) . It has two key dimensions: social
presence, the sense of interpersonal closeness and connection with the streamer, and
telepresence, the feeling of being “there” in the live streaming space. These types of
presence are critical in live  streaming commerce, where real -time interactions,
emotional cues, and content responsiveness drive stronger parasocial relationships
and authenticity perceptions (Ming et al., 2021; Liao et al., 2022; Chung et al., 2025;
Wang, Li & Yan, 2025).

69

Flow theory further supports the idea that such immersive experiences can lead to
deeper cognitive and emotional engagement. Previous research suggests that when
followers experience high levels of presence during a live streaming session, they are
more likely to emotionally engage, trust the streamer  and facilitate stronger brand
connections, and respond to calls to action , including making unplanned purchases
(Liu & Yu, 2022; Hossain et al., 2023; Xin, Liu & Jian, 202 4). These immersive
experiences lower psychological resistance  could setting the stage and creating
favourable conditions for engagement and impulse behaviour  (Abell & Biswas, 2023;
Li & Xie, 2020).

Despite increasing attention to flow in digital marketing, little is known about how
presence and flow jointly operate as mediating mechanisms between streamer
attributes and dynamic brand experiences. Most existing studies have not examined
how immersive engagement, facilitated by presence, contributes to real -time brand
connection in live  streaming commerce. This research seeks to extend Flow Theory
by exploring how presence , particularly social and telepresence , mediates the
relationship between streamers and follower behaviours. In doing so, it helps clarify
how immersive live streaming experiences enhance enjoyment, foster brand trust, and
ultimately drive engagement and impulse buying.

2.2.5 Customer Engagement Theory
In synthesising the core theoretical perspectives —Source Credibility Theory,
Congruence Theory, Flow Theory, and Consumer Engagement Theory—this research
positions Customer Engagement as the central conceptual anchor for understanding
follower behaviour in  live streaming commerce. Customer Engagement Theory has

70

emerged as a prominent perspective for understanding the nature of consumers’
cognitive, emotional, and behavioural connections with brands, products, and
organisations (Brodie et al., 2011; Hollebeek, 2011). The theory suggests that
engagement extends beyond transactional outcomes and reflects a multidimensional
psychological state characterised by varying levels of attention, participation,
interaction, and emotional involvement. In digital and socia l media environments,
customer engagement has been associated with activities such as commenting,
sharing, recommending, co -creating content, and maintaining ongoing relationships
with brands (Dessart et al., 2015). Consequently, customer engagement has become
an important concept for explaining how consumers actively participate in value
creation and develop stronger brand relationships.  While traditional marketing
approaches often rely on rational information delivery and static advertisements,
experiential marketing shifts the focus to creating immersive, emotionally resonant,
and memorable brand experiences  (Zheng, Li & Na, 2022; Hossain et al., 2023; Li,
Feng & Zhao, 2023; Lyu, Liang & Vellore Nagarajan, 2024; Luo, Xu & Zheng, 2024) .
In live streaming contexts, these experiences are shaped not only by what streamers
communicate about a product but also by how the interaction feels — dynamic,
spontaneous, and authentic.

All in all, Source Credibility Theory helps explain how trust, expertise, and
attractiveness shape the streamer’s persuasiveness, while Congruence Theory
addresses how alignment between the streamer’s image, the brand identity, and the
follower’s self -concept influenc es engagement. Meanwhile, Flow Theory and the
concept of Presence illuminate how real-time immersion and psychological connection
to the streaming environment foster emotional absorption and attention. These

71

elements work together to co-create dynamic brand experiences, which serve as the
experiential core of live streamer commerce.

Building on this, customer engagement theory conceptualises engagement as both a
psychological state—cognitive, emotional, and affective involvement (Hsu & Hu, 2023;
Li et al., 2023; Wang, 2024) , such as engagement, commenting, sharing, or
impulsively purchasing during live streaming . Unlike traditional advertising, where
engagement may be passive or delayed, live streaming enables active, reciprocal
interactions between followers, streamers, and brands  (Zheng, Li & Na, 2022; Chen,
Pham & Tran, 2024; Liu et al., 202 4). Despite this theoretical richness, few studies
have integrated these frameworks to explain the full experiential pathway from
streamer attributes through dynamic brand experiences to engagement and impulse
buying.

By bringing these theories into dialogue, this research not only addresses existing
gaps in digital marketing literature but also proposes a comprehensive, empirically
testable model of how immersive, interactive experiences in live streaming commerce
drive follower engagement and conversion. This multi -theoretical approach
strengthens the explanatory power of the study and deepens our understanding of
how streamers function as brand co-creators in digitally mediated environments.

In conclusion, these theoretical foundations collectively support the conceptual model
of this study by offering a logically integrated explanation of how live streaming
commerce operates as an interactive marketing environment. Streamers’ credible
attributes—such as expertise, trustworthiness, and attractiveness—serve as the initial

72

stimuli that activate followers’ cognitive and emotional evaluations. These evaluations
are further shaped by the perceived congruence between the brand, streamer, and
follower identities, as well as by immersive presence experiences —including both
social presence and telepresence. Together, these psychological mechanisms
facilitate the construction of dynamic brand experiences, which are at the heart of
experiential marketing. These experiences, in turn, generate higher levels of consumer
engagement and impulse buying behaviour, reflecting both psychological involvement
and observable follower actions. From a Customer Engagement Theory perspective,
engagement represents a multidimensional construct comprising cognitive, emotional,
and behavioural dimensions (Brodie et al., 2011; Hollebeek , 2011). In live streaming
commerce, engagement is reflected not only in followers’ psychological involvement
but also in observable behaviours such as commenting, sharing, interacting with
streamers, and participating in brand -related activities. As a cen tral theoretical lens,
Customer Engagement Theory helps explain how followers transform their
perceptions and experiences into active participation and behavioural responses. This
theoretical synthesis provides the justification for the hypothesised relationships within
the conceptual framework and establishes a robust foundation for the next section,
where each construct is critically reviewed, and hypotheses are proposed. To support
this theoretical integration, Appendix 6 summarises the key theories underpinning this
research and clarifies their specific relevance to the hypothesised relationships.

2.2.6 Research Gap 1: Integrating Different Theories
Despite the previous research into these elements, the literature reveals that a
comprehensive model that encapsulates the entire spectrum of the S-O-R framework
Source Credibility Theory and Congruence theory —specifically within the context of

73

live streaming —remains unexplored. Existing studies have concentrated on
quantifying engagement metrics (Wongkitrungrueng, Dehouche & Assarut, 2020;
Wymer et al., 2021; Meisner & Ledbetter, 2022; Zheng, Li & Na, 2022) and dissecting
individual elements of streamer attributes (Guo, Zhang & Wang, 2022; Law et al., 2023;
Yun et al., 2023; Zheng et al., 2023) but have yet to fully capture the interplay between
these factors. Moreover, external variables such as gender (Liu et al., 2022; Kong,
Zhan & Zhu, 2023) and product type (Guo, Zhang & Wang, 2022; Lei, Shi & Liu, 2023;
Lu, Li & Ge, 2023)  on impulse buying in the live streaming setting are not well
understood. These gaps in the literature underscore the research value of this study
and highlight the need for a more comprehensive understanding of these phenomena.

By addressing these gaps, the proposed research not only contributes to the academic
literature but also offers practical implications for marketers looking to leverage live
streaming platforms. The study aims to investigate the impact of streamer attribut es
on impulse buying and  follower engagement behaviours, considering the various
elements of the dynamic brand experience and the moderating effects of external
variables. This comprehensive approach promises to deepen the theoretical
understanding of follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour in live streaming
and provide insights that can inform digital marketing strategies and  live streaming
commerce. With live streaming platforms becoming increasingly popular, this research
is timely and holds the potential to shape future digital marketing practices and
strategies.

74

2.3 Key Constructs
2.3.1 Streamers’ Attributes
The streamers’ attributes refer to the personal temperament and characteristics
displayed when introducing and recommending products to consumers through live
streaming on social platforms. The attributes of streamers play a foundational role in
shaping how audiences perceive, engage with, and respond to live streaming content.
According to Source Credibility Theory (Hovland & Weiss, 1951), 3 core components
determine a communicator’s persuasive impact: expertise (Wiedmann & von
Mettenheim, 2020) , trustworthiness (Crnjak-Karanović, Kursan Milaković & Elez,
2023), and attractiveness (Zheng et al., 2023) . In the live streaming context, these
attributes influence followers’ perceptions of authenticity, the informational value of the
stream, their engagement and impulse buying, and each dimension, related theories
and research gaps are introduced one by one below.

2.3.1.1 Trustworthiness
Trustworthiness refers to the degree to which a source is perceived as honest, reliable,
sincere, and dependable when providing information (Ohanian, 1990; Kim & Kim,
2021). The interactive nature of live streaming means that trustworthiness is not only
established through the content but also through the streamer's behavio urs (Kim &
Kim, 2021) .  In existing studies, trustworthiness has been further dissected into
specific dimensions. For instance, Brinson & Lemon (2023)  suggest that
Trustworthiness is influenced by perceived credibility and expertise but also includes
perceived real-world feel, which is the degree to which the audience feels the content
represents an actual person or organisation. In the context of live streaming,
trustworthiness could be established through various means. The streamer's

75

consistent track record of providing accurate information could enhance their
trustworthiness  (Wellman, 2023; Kim & Kim, 2021) . Moreover, the direct and
transparent nature of live streaming can contribute to a sense of authenticity, which is
closely related to trustworthiness (Zhang et al., 2022) . Also, trustworthiness is not
static; it is a dynamic attribute that could change over time and is sensitive to the
context of the communication and the medium through which it is conveyed (Todd &
Melancon, 2017) . In the rapidly evolving landscape of digital marketing, where
misinformation could be prevalent, the trustworthiness of a live streamer becomes
critical for followers seeking reliable information and authentic interaction (Wellman,
2023; Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020). In addition, Studies show that trust in the
streamer is positively associated with both purchase intention (Yang, Feng & Wang,
2019) and follower engagement (Wellman, 2023; Wiedmann & von Mettenheim,
2020). As followers seek authenticity in streamer content, streamers who behave
consistently and transparently tend to foster stronger emotional bonds and trust.

2.3.1.2 Attractiveness
Attractiveness represents physical appearance in live streaming context, which is an
essential dimension that influences an online streamer's perceived credibility. Also,
some researchers have argued that attractiveness refers to the extent to which the
streamer is seen as likeable, appealing, and relatable by their followers. This
dimension goes beyond physical attractiveness and includes personality traits and the
overall persona projected by the streamer (Ooi et al., 2023).

Current studies show the streamers' attraction in the live streaming context is
paramount (Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020; Kim & Park, 2023) ; it involves the

76

streamer's ability to establish a rapport with the audience (Giertz et al., 2021) , often
through shared interests, interactive dialogue, and a compelling on -screen presence.
Physical attractiveness could play a role in live streaming, but it is often superseded
by the authenticity and relatability of the streamer (Kim & Kim, 2021). Audiences tend
to move towards streamers who exhibit genuine behaviour and a sense of 'being
themselves' on camera, which could foster trust and a stronger parasocial
relationship—a sense of friendship or emotional connection with a media persona (Kim
& Park, 2023; Zheng, Wu & Liao, 2023) . On the other hand, the immediacy of live
streaming also adds a layer of attraction based on the streamer's responsiveness to
their audience (Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020) . For example, the ability to
interact and engage with viewers in real-time, answering questions and acknowledging
comments, could create a more dynamic and personable experience, further
enhancing the streamer's attractiveness (Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020; Wang
et al., 2021; Liao et al., 2022; Kim & Park, 2023).

2.3.1.3 Expertise
Expertise encapsulates the perceived knowledge (Feng, Chen & Kong, 2021; Liao et
al., 2022), skills (Liao et al., 2022; Zheng et al., 2023), and abilities (Li et al., 2023) of
a communicator within a specific domain, and it is a critical factor in determining the
effectiveness of their influence on an audience's attitudes and behaviours. Expertise
is not inherently linked to the actual knowledge the communicator possesses bu t is
rather the audience's perception of the communicator's capability to provide accurate
and valuable information (Park & Lin, 2020; Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020; Gao
et al., 2021). This perception is often influenced by the communicator's professional
experience (Cartwright, Liu & Davies, 2022)  and the presentation of information in a

77

clear and cogent manner, which could either bolster or diminish the communicator's
persuasive impact (Borchers & Enke, 2021) . Expertise is especially significant in
situations where the audience lacks the knowledge to evaluate the content
independently and must rely on the communicator's authority on the subject (Church,
2022).

The current studies show, the streamer's perceived expertise and competence c ould
significantly influence viewers' perceptions and lead to higher credibility and influence
over the follower (Church, 2022). Expertise not only bolsters their credibility but also
adds substantial value to the viewer experience by providing insightful and
authoritative content (Chen & Yang, 2023). Together, these attributes are the bedrock
upon which a streamer's influence is built, determining their ability to attract, engage,
and retain an audience in the dynamic and interactive realm of live streaming. (Syrdal
et al., 2023; Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020).

2.3.1.4  Streamers’ Attributes and Other Factors
Streamers’ attributes have been extensively studied across various domains, including
sense of presence, brand follower streamer congruence (Abell & Biswas, 2023; Chen,
Yan & Smith, 2023) and dynamic brand experiences (Addo et al., 2021; Bu, Parkinson
& Thaichon, 2022; Deng, Lin & Jiang, 2023) . These attributes have been scrutinised
in the context of live streaming commerce and the burgeoning field of live streaming
(Addo et al., 2021; Chen & Yang, 2023; Leong et al., 2023) . For example, Li et al.
(2022) examined how the attractiveness of a live streamer could influence viewer
dynamic brand experiences. This research discovered that viewers tend to engage
longer with streamers they find aesthetically appealing. Similarly, the authenticity of

78

the live streamer has been linked to audience trust and subsequent follower loyalty
(Zhang et al., 2022), while credibility has been associated with product persuasiveness
and the effectiveness of brand endorsements during live streams (Yang, Feng &
Wang, 2019; Kim & Kim, 2021) . Expertise, on the other hand, has been shown to
enhance the informational value of live streaming content and brand, thereby
increasing the educational impact on viewers (Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, 2020) .
Each of these attributes play s a crucial role in the complex dynamics of viewer
interaction and behaviour within the live streaming space.

Together, these attributes form the credibility profile of the streamer and significantly
influence how followers interpret live content. In particula r credibility, attractive, and
expert streamer is more likely to deliver a compelling sense of presence, brand
follower streamer congruence —real-time, emotionally resonant, and information-rich
moments that shape how consumers engage with the brand (Wiedmann & von
Mettenheim, 2020).

2.3.1.5 Research Gap 2: Streamer Attributes Sense of Presence and Congruence
Streamer attributes such as  attractiveness, expertise, and credibility have been
analysed in the context of live streaming and online interactions. For instance,
research by Li & Xie (2020)  demonstrates that viewers tend to associate streamer
attractiveness with higher -quality content and brand. Similarly, investigations into
authenticity, such as those by Kanaveedu & Kalapurackal (2022), show that streamers
who exhibit genuine behaviour tend to foster a more dedicated following. Some studies
have also delved into the impact of credibility and expertise on viewer behaviour. For
example, Lou & Yuan (2019)  have shown that streamers who are perceived as

79

credible or expert during their live streaming not only attract more viewers but also
maintain higher engagement rates. In the same area, other research focusing on
expertise, like the work by Wiedmann & von Mettenheim, (2020) , has found that
streamers with specialised knowledge in a subject can command more influence and
are more effective in promoting products or brands. In terms of credibility, also work
by Chen (2019) has also shown that viewers are more likely to be persuaded by and
purchase from streamers they find credible. This is in line with research by Kim et al.
(2020), which points out that credibility could be a major driver behind effective product
endorsements in live streaming. Expertise plays a crucial role, as indicated by Davis
and Park (2020), who find that streamers with specialised knowledge could command
greater influence, persuade followers and promote brands more effectively.

Despite these insights, the research linking these streamers’ attributes directly to the
sense of presence and congruence in live streaming is not as well-established. While
attractiveness, authenticity, credibility, and expertise are individually recognised for
their impact on viewer engagement, there is a gap in understanding how these
attributes collectively contribute to  a sense of presence and congruence during live
streams. For example, while some studies have explored the role of credibility and
expertise (Wang et al., 2022) , there is less clarity on how these attributes interact to
create the sense of presence . This indicates a research gap in understanding how
various streamer attributes collectively influence the sense of presence and
congruence in live streaming. Also, in the field of live streaming commerce, there's an
ongoing debate regarding the impact of streamer attributes —such as attractiveness,
authenticity, credibility, and expertise —on the sense of presence and congruence .
Discussions in this area often revolve around whether streamers who showcase more

80

natural and authentic behaviours (emphasising authenticity and credibility) or those
who present a more polished and engaging persona (highlighting attractiveness and
expertise) are more effective in influencing the sense of presence and congruence.
Also, research so far presents mixed outcomes. Some studies suggest that streamers
who are engaging tend to initially draw more viewers (Chen, Yan & Smith, 2023) . In
contrast, others emphasise the long -term impact of credibility in maintaining viewer
interest and fostering a deeper connection with the brand (Wang et al., 2022), bringing
more congruence. Taken together, these findings suggest that while single attributes
may generate short -term attention , credibility is likely more influential in sustaining
followers’ trust and cultivating an enduring sense of presence and congruence.

According to those arguments, future research should focus on the comprehensive
role these attributes play in shaping viewer perceptions and behaviours in the evolving
landscape of live streaming, moving beyond individual traits to explore the synergistic
effect they have on creating im pactful immersive experiences. In addition, while the
attributes of streamers are recognised for their influence on customer behaviour, the
overall effect of these combined attributes on shaping the sense of presence and
congruence is not fully understood. Therefore, this research seeks to contribute to this
discussion by providing empirical evidence on how different combinations of live
streamer attributes affect the sense of presence and the congruence is not fully
understood.

2.3.2 Sense of Presence
The sense of presence refers to the feeling of being immersed and actively involved
in a digital environment, giving users the experience of being physically present in a

81

non-physical world (Chen & Wu, 2023; Onderdijk et al., 2023; Yan, Wu & Meng, 2023).
There are some different aspects of presence: social presence and telepresence
(Lavoye et al., 2023). Social presence is characterised by Snelson (2019) the degree
of salience between two communicators in a communication setting . Over the years,
the relationship between different forms of presence and engagement has been
thoroughly examined. Each presence is explained in detail below.

2.3.2.1 Social Presence
Social presence is the sense of being with others and the perception of personal, social
interaction within a communication environment, even when the participants are not
physically co-located (Ma, Zou & Lv, 2022; You, Wang & Shamu, 2023).

In existing research, there's a spirited discourse surrounding the impact of social
presence in social media and live streaming on follower engagement. A faction of
academia proposes that heightened social presence could elevate follower
engagement (Ye, Zheng & Li, 2022) and brand experiences. For example Sang et al.
(2023), analysed users who watched live streaming, and it was found that social
presence moderated the effect of source credibility on continued viewing intentions,
with stronger social presence. The higher user engagement, while a contrasting
perspective suggest s otherwise , argues that social presence has no significant
positive effect on follower engagement and brand experiences . For example, Deng,
Lin & Jiang, (2023) analysing the relationship between consumer social presence and
followers in e -commerce live streaming through 400 questionnaires concluded that
social presence has no discernible impact on  brand experiences as well as  follower
engagement. The dialogue on social presence and its consequent effects on follower

82

engagement and credibility remains open -ended, as delineated by these varied
standpoints, because no same result.

In addition, some research has shown that it has been well documented that
immersive live streaming experiences increase interaction trust (Abell & Biswas, 2023;
Li & Xie, 2020) and brand experiences. This engagement is attributed to the ease with
which users could interact with streamers and content (Wongkitrungrueng & Assarut,
2020). Prior studies have delved into how different social media post designs, such as
evaluating content quality (Ki & Kim, 2019) , content format (Li & Xie, 2020) , and
comparing brand sponsored and non - brand sponsored content (Lou, Tan & Chen,
2019),  influence user engagement. However, the impact of sense of presence  and
dynamic brand experiences  in the context of live streaming versus the absence of
relevant information on follower engagement and impulse buying remains largely
unexplored. Given this gap in research and the evolving live streaming strategies
employed by brands across platforms, there is an evident demand for a deeper
exploration in this domain.

2.3.2.2 Telepresence
Telepresence delves into the subjective experience of being in one place or
environment, even when physically situated in another ( Tjostheim, Leister &
Waterworth 2019) . Past research has examined the relationship between
telepresence and follower engagement, often identifying an array of mediating
variables. Variables such as trust (Wongkitrungrueng & Assarut, 202 0) and flow
experiences (Ye, Zheng & Li, 2022) have been frequently discussed. It is important to
emphasise that there are many factors that cause followers' engagement, aspects like

83

interactivity, and brand experiences stand out, for example, studies have found that
the more frequently the streamer interacts with the follower during a live streaming the
higher the level of good brand experiences and consumer engagement, also they have
found that hedonic content is effective in increasing consumer engagement (Hughes,
Swaminathan & Brooks, 2019) . Also, well-researched and factually accurate content
can boost perceived expertise. Earlier studies indicate that these factors play a
mediating role in the live -streaming domain. However, previous research has not
explored how these factors affect dynamic bra nd experiences as well as mediate the
relationship between streamer s’ attributes and dynamic brand experiences in the
context of telepresence. In addition, numerous studies suggest that telepresence
could enhance the depth of user immersion in a virtual environment, subsequently
impacting brand experience engagement levels (Barta, Gurrea & Flavián, 2023) .
Exploring this telepresence could uncover unique aspects of live streaming  that are
not encapsulated by current understandings rooted in the social presence or
telepresence research. Therefore, their impact needs to be further explored.

2.3.2.3 Research Gap 3: Sense of Presence and Dynamic Brand Experiences
The sense of presence in the realm of live streaming is defined by the perceptual
attributes that allow followers to feel immersed within the virtual environment being
streamed (Ye, Zheng & Li, 2022) . This can be influenced by factors such as the
vividness of the environment being portrayed (Sang et al., 2023) , the symmetry and
Impressive content (Wies, Bleier & Edeling, 2022) , and the diversity of interactive
elements (Kang et al., 2021).

84

In addition, taking into consideration the evolving research landscape in live streaming
commerce, it becomes apparent that the integration of social presence, telepresence
and dynamic brand experiences in content presentation profoundly impacts follower
engagement and impulse buying. Previous studies have elucidated that a compelling
presentation is important in fostering follower engagement (Ye, Zheng & Li, 2022;
Sang et al., 2023) . Moreover, the degree of interactivity embedded within credibility
plays a transformative role in sustaining and amplifying follower engagement (Yang,
Feng & Wang, 2019) . This means that when streamers not only appear credible —
through expertise, authenticity, and trustworthiness —but also actively engage with
followers in real time, their credibility becomes more persuasive and relational rather
than static. Interactive cr edibility, demonstrated through immediate responses,
personalised communication, and transparent demonstrations, reinforces followers’
trust and emotional connection, thereby sustaining long -term engagement and
encouraging repeated interactions. Those are also characteristics of telepresence.

However, while prior studies have recognised telepresence as a key psychological
state that evokes the feeling of “being there” in a mediated environment (Steuer, 1992;
He, Li & Xue, 2022; Li & Xie, 2020) , its underlying mechanisms and influence within
live streaming commerce remain underexplored. Telepresence is shaped by
vividness, interactivity, and immersion —factors that together determine how
consumers perceive their virtual proximity to the streamer and the product being
presented. Existing research primarily examines telepresence as a technological or
sensory construct (He, Li & Xue, 2022), yet few studies have unpacked how it interacts
with human elements such as streamer credibility, authenticity, and communication

85

style to create dynamic brand experiences. (Gao, Jiang & Guo, 2023; Khoi, Le &
Nguyen Dong, 2023; Lu, He & Ke, 2023; Men, Zheng & Davison, 2023)

In particular, there  is limited understanding of how telepresence mediates the
relationship between streamer attributes and consumer outcomes —specifically, how
immersive experiences amplify emotional involvement, cognitive absorption, and
impulsive decision-making. While some studies link telepresence to engagement and
purchase intention (Li & Xue, 2022; Li & Xie, 2020; Hugh et al. 2022), they often isolate
it from related constructs such as congruence or dynamic brand experience.
Therefore, this research addresses a theoretical gap by situating telepresence within
an integrative model that connects streamer attributes, sense of presence,  and
dynamic brand experience to follower engagement and impulse buying. By
conceptualising telepresence not merely as a technological by -product but as a
psychologically mediated experience, this study extends existing literature and
provides a deeper und erstanding of how immersive interactions shape follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviour in live streaming commerce.

2.3.3 Congruence
In the evolving landscape of live streaming, the congruence among brands, streamers,
and followers stands as a critical factor for marketing success (Ma, 2021; Hudders, De
Jans & De Veirman, 2021; Giertz et al., 2021; Bawack et al., 2023; Dua, 2023). Brand,
Streamer, and Follower Congruence refer to the harmony and alignment between the
values, interests, and expectations of the three entities. This alignment is crucial in
ensuring a cohesive and authentic experience that resonates well with the audience,
strengthens brand loyalty, and willingness to engage (Ma, 2021; Tian & Li, 2023; Yeh,

86

2023; Picot-Coupey et al., 2023; Shao, 2023) . Each of the different congruences is
explained in detail below.

2.3.3.1 Brand-Streamer Congruence
The alignment between the brand's values and the streamer's persona is fundamental
(Liu, Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Ko, 2023; Li et al., 2023) . Current researches illustrate
that w hen a streamer's style, content, and values mirror that of the brand, the
endorsement appears more genuine and credible (Hughes, Swaminathan & Brooks,
2019; Ashraf, Hameed & Saeed, 2023; Liu et al., 2023; Ooi et al., 2023; Dam, Borsai
& Burroughs, 2024) . Meanwhile, this congruence strengthens the brand's message,
as the streamer effectively becomes an extension of the brand, embodying its ethos
and appealing to a like-minded audience. Research indicates that such alignment can
significantly impact the audience's perception of the brand, leading to higher trust and
a stronger brand image and engagement (Park & Lin, 2020; Xue & Liu, 2022; Arora et
al., 2023; Li & Nan, 2023; Bawack et al., 2023).

2.3.3.2 Streamer-Follower Congruence
The relationship between streamers and their followers is built on a foundation of
shared interests and values (Liu, Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Ma, 2021; Wu et al., 2022;
Yang et al., 2023) . Current research has found  that when followers perceive a
streamer as someone with similar tastes, opinions, and a relatable lifestyle, they are
more likely to trust and engage with the content  and brand. This perceived similarity
fosters a strong parasocial relationship, where followers feel a personal connection to
the streamer, making the streamer's endorsements more persuasive and impactful

87

(Hughes, Swaminathan & Brooks, 2019; Ma, 2021; Ozuem & Willis, 2022; Caruelle,
2023).

2.3.3.3 Brand-Follower Congruence
For followers, the alignment of their own values and preferences with the brand's
identity is crucial (Sánchez-Fernández & Jiménez-Castillo, 2021; Cabeza-Ramírez et
al., 2022; Caruelle, 2023; Shao, 2023) . Followers are more receptive to brands that
they perceive as reflecting their lifestyle and beliefs. When this congruence is present,
followers are more likely to develop a positive attitude towards the brand, exhibit
stronger brand loyalty, and engage i n word -of-mouth promotion (Wei et al., 2022;
Caruelle, 2023) . The streamer, in this context, acts as a bridge, reinforcing this
congruence through tailored content and personal endorsements (Ma, 2021; Cabeza-
Ramírez et al., 2022; Wei et al., 2022).

All in all, Brand, Streamer, and Follower Congruence is a dynamic and influential factor
in the realm of live streaming commerce (Shan, Chen & Lin, 2020; Ma, 2021; Pradhan,
Kishore & Gokhale, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023) . However, current research focuses on
individual relationship between followers and streamers or brands and followers. No
research has yet integrated followers, streamers, and brands in live streaming
commerce. However, studies have shown that the alignment of values, interests, and
expectations among the three entities enhances the authenticity and effectiveness of
brand endorsements, fosters deeper relationships, and drives engagement and brand
loyalty (Wei et al., 2022; Caruelle, 2023) . Understanding and navigating this
congruence is essential for brands, streamers, and marketers aiming to create
impactful and resonant marketing campaigns in the digital age. (Feng, Chen & Kong,

88

2021; Leite & Baptista, 2021; Ma, 2021; Cabeza-Ramírez et al., 2022; Alboqami, 2023;
Ooi et al., 2023; Nash, 2023).

2.3.3.4 Research Gap 4: Congruence and Dynamic Brand Experience
The congruence between brand, streamer, and follower is increasingly recognised in
contemporary marketing literature as an important element shaping consumer
experiences and behaviours, particularly within interactive contexts like live streaming.
Congruence theory posits that the alignment or fit among the identities, values, and
personalities of brands, streamers, and their audience significantly strengthens
consumer perceptions and subsequent behaviours (Abosag et al., 2020; Rollin, 2022;
Arora et al., 2023). Specifically, brand-streamer congruence involves the compatibility
between the brand’s image and the streamer's persona, while streamer -follower
congruence addresses the similarity or alignment of values, lifestyles, and identities
between streamers and their audience (Deng, Chen & Wu, 2024; Opić & Pandža Bajs,
2024; Roth-Cohen, Segev & Liu, 2024).

Recent literature consistently underscores the importance of streamer -follower
congruence as a mediator of consumer behaviour (Venciute et al., 2023; Ceylan &
Hayran, 2025) . When followers perceive high congruence with a streamer, they
experience heightened trust, stronger emotional connections, and increased
receptivity to brand -related messages, facilitating more meaningful dynamic brand
experiences (Venciute et al., 2023; Supriani, Ninglasari & Iswati, 2024). This relational
alignment reduces psychological distance, making followers feel more intimately
connected and aligned with the streamer’s recommendations, thus positively

89

influencing their engagement levels and enhancing their likelihood of impulsive
purchase behaviours (Liang & Yoon, 2022; Zhang & Hu, 2024).

However, while existing studies emphasise the importance of congruence in
influencing consumer outcomes, there are theoretical gaps that persist.  First, limited
research explicitly examines the role of streamer–follower congruence as a mediating
mechanism that links streamer attributes, such as expertise and credibility, to brand
experiences within live streaming commerce. Although previous studies have
examined congruence between brands and endorsers in traditional influencer
marketing (e.g., Wang, 2024; Ji, Liu & Chen, 2023), the unique characteristics of live
streaming—such as immediacy, spontaneous communication, and real -time
interaction—introduce new dynamics to how congruence forms and operates. In live
streaming environments, congruence is not static but co -created through continuous
interaction between streamers and followers, evolving as followers respond to the
streamer’s behaviour, tone, and authenticity in real time. This interactive process may
amplify or diminish followers’ sense of identification and thus directly affect
engagement and impulse buying behaviour. Despite these distinctive dynamics,
current literature has not adequately ex plored how streamer –follower congruence
functions as a mediating mechanism in shaping dynamic brand experiences.

Second, some research has often treated congruence between streamer and follower
as a static construct rather than a dynamic and evolving aspect of consumer -brand
relationships, failing to account for how congruence perceptions may fluctuate or
intensify during interactive live streaming sessions. Given that real -time engagement
could amplify perceptions of congruence through immediate feedback and community-

90

building interactions (Deng, Chen & Wu, 2024; Lyu, Liang & Vellore Nagarajan, 2024;
Xin, Liu & Jian, 2024), there is considerable room to investigate how streamer-follower
congruence shapes dynamic brand experiences, particularly its indirect effect on
follower engagement and impulse buying behaviours during live streaming.

Addressing these gaps, the current research intends to investigate brand streamer -
follower congruence as a mediator in the relationship between streamer attributes and
the dynamic brand experience. By integrating congruence theory into the broader
framework of live streaming commerce, this study aims to provide deeper insights into
the congruency between streamer and followers’ engagement and impulse buying
behaviours, offering valuable theoretical and practical implications for brands and
digital marketers seeking more effective strategies within live streaming contexts.

2.3.4 Dynamic Brand Experiences
Dynamic brand experience builds upon the broader brand experience literature,
which conceptualises consumer experiences through sensory, affective, behavioural,
and intellectual dimensions (Brakus et al., 2009). While the concept was not
originally developed within live streaming commerce, recent studies have
emphasised the increasingly dynamic, interactive, and real-time nature of brand
experiences in digital environments (Wiedmann et al., 2018; Kumar & Kaushik,
2020). In the context of live streaming commerce, dynamic brand experience reflects
the real-time, interactive, and evolving experiences followers have during live
streaming sessions. These elements collectively contribute to a comprehensive and
immersive experience that engages followers deeply and dynamically.

91

Unlike traditional brand experiences, dynamic brand experiences in live streaming
are characterised by continuous interaction, real-time feedback, and evolving
follower participation. In the context of live streaming, dynamic brand experience is a
multifaceted concept involving various elements that collectively contribute to a
comprehensive and immersive experience. These elements—affective, sensory,
behavioural, and intellectual—interact to create an environment that engages
followers deeply and dynamically. Each element is explained in detail below.
2.3.4.1 Sensory
The sensory experience in live streaming is vital for attracting and maintaining viewer
attention (Xu, Huang & Shang, 2021; Ozuem & Willis, 2022; Wei & Xi, 2023) . It
involves stimulating the visual and auditory senses of the audience through high -
quality visuals, compelling audio, and interactive elements to influence the behaviour
of followers.

Also, streamers often use vivid imagery, live demonstrations, and engaging narratives
to make the experience more immersive (Park, Hong & Park, 2023) . The sensory
appeal not only enhances the perception of the brand but also makes the experience
more memorable and enjoyable for the followers (Ozuem & Willis, 2022; Park, Hong
& Park, 2023) . A well -crafted streaming environment that is visually appealing
encourages viewers to stay longer and interact more with the stream and influences
followers’ purchasing decisions (Paraman et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023).

2.3.4.2 Affective
Affective plays a crucial role in shaping the brand experience. Streamers who can
evoke feelings of excitement, joy, trust, or even nostalgia create a stronger emotional

92

bond with their followers (Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Li et al., 2023; Luo et al.,
2023). When viewers experience positive emotions during a live stream, such as
excitement or joy, they are more likely to participate actively, express their opinions,
and share content with their networks (Lim et al., 2020; Guo, Zhang & Wang, 2022;
Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023; Shao, 2023) . This affective resonance could significantly
influence followers' perceptions of the brand and their willingness to engage with it
(Zhang, Wang & Zhang, 2021; Dang -Van et al., 2023)  even influence their
behaviourally responsive. So positive emotions elicited during the streaming sessions
could lead to a more favourable brand attitude and higher chances of impulse buying
(Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023).

2.3.4.3 Behavioural
Behavioural, as a dimension of dynamic brand experiences, is posited to have a
positive impact on follower engagement behaviour. The behaviour between streamers
and their follower is a fundamental aspect of the live streaming experience (Gu, Cheng
& Shen, 2023; Hou & Hou, 2024) . When followers feel a personal connection to the
streamer, their engagement behaviour is likely to increase, as they perceive the
streamer not just as a content provider but as a social entity with whom they can
interact and relate (Hou et al., 2019; Liao et al., 2022; Liu & Yu, 2022; Shao, 2023).

Current research illustrates that interaction is one of the key factors affecting consumer
decisions and behaviours. The behavioural aspect of the brand experience in live
streaming centres around the connections formed between the brand, streamer, and
followers. These relationships are cultivated through interactive and personalised
engagement behaviour, fostering the sense of community and belonging of followers.

93

At the same time, it could influence the behaviour of followers (Wang, 2021; Ozuem &
Willis, 2022; Kalam, Goi & Tiong, 2023; Li et al., 2023) . Also, streamers often act as
the medium through which followers form a parasocial relationship with the brand,
making the interaction feel more personal and direct (Kalam, Goi & Tiong, 2023;
Tanwar, Chaudhry & Srivastava, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023; Huang & Ma, 2024) . This
sense of connection is fundamental to a dynamic brand experience, as it encourages
continued engagement behaviour and decision-making (Ozuem & Willis, 2022; Liu et
al., 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023).

2.3.4.4 Intellectual
Intellectual refers to how followers process information, form perceptions, and make
decisions regarding the brand (Wang et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023) . This includes
awareness, understanding, and memory of the brand messages conveyed during the
live streaming. Streamers who can effectively communicate the brand's values,
features, and benefits in an understandable and relatable manner contribute to a more
informed and educated audience (Akrout & Nagy, 2018; Bu, Parkinson & Thaichon,
2022). The current study found that intellectual engagement is essential for followers
to make informed decisions, behavioural responsive , and develop a deeper
understanding of the brand (Yin et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023; Sohaib & Han, 2023).

Furthermore, the previous research also found that the intellectual elicited by dynamic
brand experiences is hypothesised to positively affect impulse buying. When
streamers successfully communicate the value and benefits of a product, they c an
influence viewers' beliefs and attitudes, making them more inclined to make decisions
and behavioural respons es (Yin et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2022) . So this intellectual

94

aspect could be particularly persuasive when viewers are presented with compelling
reasons or narratives that justify the buying behaviour and then impact followers’
decision making.

All in all, Dynamic brand experience refers to the real-time, interactive, and emotionally
charged encounters consumers have with brands during live streaming
(Wiedmann et al., 2018; Kumar & Kaushik, 2020; Yu et al., 2021; Zha et al., 2022; Li
et al., 2023; Park, Hong & Park, 2023; Wang et al., 2023) . Unlike traditional static
brand experiences, live streaming allows brands to engage followers through visual,
auditory, and conversational cues — creating a more immersive and memorable brand
interaction(Iglesias, Markovic & Rialp, 2019; Khan et al., 2020; Kumar & Kaushik, 2020;
Tran & Chang, 2022) . Prior studies suggest that such experiences not only enhance
brand recall and emotional connection but also influence engagement and unplanned
buying behaviour  (Iglesias, Markovic & Rialp, 2019; Khan et al., 2020; Kumar &
Kaushik, 2020; Tran & Chang, 2022) . However, most existing research has focused
on outcome variables without fully exploring how dynamic brand experience functions
as a bridge between streamer attributes and follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour. There remains a gap in understanding its mediating role in shaping follower
engagement and impulse buying.

2.3.4.5 Research Gap 5: Dynamic Brand Experiences , Engagement and Impulse
Buying
Furthermore, recent systematic review  highlights that brand experiences in live
streaming significantly influence followers' engagement and buying behavio ur,
particularly through real-time interactions, sensory stimulation, emotional resonance,

95

and cognitive engagement (Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023; Li, Xuan
& Chen, 2024; Sarker & Dwivedi, 2024) , Also existing studies emphasise how
interactions between streamers and followers —such as live product demonstrations,
personalised content, and immediate more audience participant and enhance
impulsive purchase decisions (Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong,
2023; Wei & Xi, 2023).

However, although the existing literature acknowledges the importance of these
experiential components, a theoretical gap persists regarding the specific mechanisms
through which dynamic brand experiences trigger follower engagement and impulse
buying during live streams. While previous studies have identified the roles such as
emotional, affective and intellectual experiences in shaping consumer attitudes, most
have treated these factors as isolated dimensions rather than examining how they
interact dynamically in real time to drive engagement and impulse buying (Khoi, Le &
Nguyen Dong, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023; Li, Xuan & Chen, 2024). Consequently, it remains
unclear how the affective and intellectual responses cultivated within live streams
translate into measurable follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour.

Furthermore, there remains limited exploration of how different elements of dynamic
brand experience stimuli affect follower engagement and impulse buying.  Also,
previous research has suggested that the indirect relationship between streamers’
attributes, sense of presence and streamer follower congruence with follower
engagement and impulse buying has been ignored (Zheng, Li & Na, 2022; Gu, Cheng
& Shen, 2023; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023; Li, Xuan & Chen,
2024). This research gap presents an opportunity for further theoretical advancement,

96

especially regarding how these dynamic brand experience elements for such as
sensory, affective, intellectual, and behaviour, interplay with streamer attributes and
brand streamer follower congruence, as well as sense of presence to directly and
indirectly influence follower engagement and impulse buying in live streaming
contexts.

2.4 Followers Engagement
In the live streaming  area, dynamic brand experiences are important in cultivating
follower engagement. Existing systematic review  shows that the richness of these
experiences could boost the level of interaction and involvement of followers during
live streams (Yadav et al., 2026) . Most of the research is supported by engagement
theory, which posits that engaging digital environments encourage s active
participation (Fredricks, Blumenfeld, & Paris, 2004).

Sense of presence, brand follower streamer congruence  and dynamic brand
experiences are theorised to positively influence followers’ engagement. The
multidimensional nature of these experiences, encompassing affective, sensory,
behavioural, and Intellectual elements, provides an immersive platform where
followers are not just passive viewers but active participants. The stimulus provided
by the dynamic brand experience in live streaming is anticipated to enhance the
engagement levels of followers, prompting deeper interaction with the content, the
streamer, and the brand.

First, the sense of presence could positively influence follower engagement. The more
interaction in the live streaming takes place can captivate followers, making the

97

experience more immersive and engaging (Hou et al., 2019; Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023;
Ma, 2024). A well -crafted social streaming environment that encourages viewers to
stay longer and interact more with the stream (Paraman et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023).

Second, Brand, Streamer and Follower Congruence refer to the harmony and
alignment between the values, interests, and expectations of the three entities. This
alignment is crucial in ensuring a cohesive and authentic experience that resonates
well with the audience, strengthens brand loyalty, and willingness to engage (Ma, 2021;
Tian & Li, 2023; Yeh, 2023; Picot-Coupey et al., 2023; Shao, 2023)..

Third, dynamic brand experiences are theorised to positively influence follower
engagement. The usability, interactivity, and aesthetic appeal of the streaming
platform play a significant role in shaping the user experience (Yu & Zheng, 2021; Xu,
Kapitan & Phillips, 2023). A well-designed interface that facilitates easy interaction and
provides a pleasant viewing experience can enhance followers' willingness to engage
with the stream (Xiao et al., 2022; Lu, Li & Ge, 2023; Huang et al., 2024).

All in all,  the live streaming environment works synergistically to elevate the level of
follower engagement. This collective impact is crucial for brand s, streamers and
followers seeking to harness the power of live streaming to build strong relationships.
By fostering a digital environment that is relationally rich, immersive, emotionally
resonant, and well -designed, streamers and brands  could significantly boost
engagement levels, turning viewers into active participants and buyers.

98

2.5 Impulse Buying
Impulse buying, in digital commerce settings as unplanned, spontaneous purchases
often driven by emotional arousal and reduced cognitive control (Yan et al., 2022; Miin
et al., 2023; Qu et al., 2023; Luo et al., 2024). The literature provides various definitions,
generally converging on the concept that impulse buying is characterised by an abrupt,
emotion-driven decision to buy, typically occurring with little or no premeditation (Yan
et al., 2022; Miin et al., 2023; Qu et al., 2023; Luo et al., 2024) . It is an immediate
response to a stimulus, often leading to a sense of gratification or fulfilment of an
emotional need (Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024).

In live streaming contexts, impulse buying is facilitated by the sense of presence, such
as real -time interaction between streamers and followers. The immediate and
interactive environment, combined with the persuasive influence of streamers, could
create a potent trigger for impulsive purchases (Gao et al., 2021; Yan et al., 2022; Luo
et al., 2024). The measurement of impulse buying in this setting may include tracking
the number of purchases made during or immediately after a live streaming, as well
as analysing chat logs for indications of spontaneous buying language or sentiment
(Li et al., 2022; Lyu, Qi & Liu, 2022; Du & Huang, 2023; Li, Dou & Yu, 2023) . As the
digital landscape evolves, the ways in which impulse buying is both defined and
measured continue to be refined, accommodating new forms of consumer behaviour
and the various platforms on which they occur.

The brand follower and streamer congruence also positively influences impulse buying.
When followers feel a sense of connection with the streamer brand, the trust and
rapport built through these relationships can lower the psychological barriers that

99

typically inhibit impulse buying (Shi, Li & Hu, 2023). This interpersonal connection and
congruence serve as a powerful motivator for viewers to make on-the-spot purchases,
often driven by the desire to emulate or please the streamer they admire
(Wongkitrungrueng & Assarut, 2020; Cheung et al., 2022).

The dynamic brand experience is also hypothesised to positively influence impulse
buying. Some empirical evidence shows that highly emotionally charged environments
— especially those involving perceived social proof- trigger impulse purchases (Zhang,
Cheng & Huang, 2023; Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024) . Emotions elicited during a live
streaming, such as excitement or trustful, could lower cognitive resistance and
encourage viewers to make immediate purchases (Pham et al., 2023; Sun & Bao,
2023; Ye & Ching, 2023) . The emotional engagement that streamers foster can thus
be a potent driver of impulse buying, as viewers seek to sustain the positive feelings
associated with the live streaming experience (Chen et al., 2021; Lee & Chen, 2021).

2.6 Gender
In this study, gender refers to the gender of followers. In previous live streaming
studies, there are many factors that could affect the relationship between
participants, and gender is one of them. Previous research found that gender could
strengthen or weaken the effects of the relationship between dynamic brand
experience and follower engagement in different ways. Gender differences in values,
interests, and purchasing behaviours could influence how individuals respond to
brand experiences.  In addition, Gender differences in processing information and
engaging with content have been observed in consumer research, suggesting that
male and female viewers might respond differently to the same brand stimuli (Li et

100

al., 2023; Rasool, Ul Islam & Shah, 2023; Zhai & Chen, 2023). For instance, the
social and relational aspects of live streaming may resonate more with one gender,
affecting their level of engagement.

Some research shows that gender might moderate the link between brand experience
and impulse buying (Yu et al., 2021; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Luo et al., 2023).
The brand experience needs to be particularly immersive and engaging to justify the
price and encourage impulse purchases (Sang et al., 2023) . Gender considerations
could also dictate the approach, tone, and content of the brand experience to better
connect with the target demographic, thereby influencing the level of Impulse Buying
(Liu et al., 2022; Kong, Zhan & Zhu, 2023).

In addition, the literature suggests that impulse buying tendencies could be influenced
by gender-based preferences and socialisation, which could mean that dynamic brand
experiences might lead to different impulse buying patterns among male and female
viewers (Yin et al., 2022; Park, Hong & Park, 2023; Rathore et al., 2024) .
Understanding these differences could help streamers and brands tailor their content
and interaction styles to resonate more deeply with different segments of their
followers (Liu et al., 2022; Kong, Zhan & Zhu, 2023).

2.7 Product Type
Prior research also found that the relationship between dynamic brand experiences
and followers’ engagement in live streaming is moderated by the type of product being
featured (Liu & Yu, 2022; Li, Tang & Feng, 2023; Peng, Ren & Guo, 2023) . For
instance, the type of product being promoted (e.g., utilitarian vs. hedonic) could also

101

moderate the impact of dynamic brand experiences (Luo et al., 2024) . Hedonic
products, which are often associated with pleasure and subjective experiences, a
strong dynamic brand experience might be more influential in driving engagement and
impulse purchases. In contrast, for utilitarian products, practicality and usefulnes s
might be the primary drivers, with the brand experience playing a supporting role.
Therefore, this study used product type as a moderator to analyse the moderating
effect of dynamic brand experience on follower engagement and impulse buying.

Also, in the relationship between dynamic brand experience and impulse purchase.
The impulse to purchase spontaneously in a live streaming context could also be
moderated by gender and product type (Kala Kamdjoug, 2023; Khoi, Le & Nguyen
Dong, 2023) . Some products might require a high level of trust and emotional
connection, typically achieved through a compelling brand experience, to prompt an
impulse buy (Wang et al., 2022; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Park, Hong & Park,
2023; Wang et al., 2022) . Meanwhile, for more affordable items, the threshold for
engagement and purchase might be lower. Product type further complicates this
relationship, as different groups might have varying responses to the same brand
experience based on their preferences, needs, and purchasing habits (Khoi, Le &
Nguyen Dong, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023).

All in all, understanding the role of moderators like gender and product type is crucial
in live streaming commerce (Todd & Melancon, 2017; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023).
These factors help explain the conditions under which dynamic brand experiences are
most effective in driving follower engagement and impulse purchases. By tailoring
strategies to account for these moderators, marketers could optimise their live

102

streaming commerce to resonate more deeply with their followers and achieve better
outcomes. In addition, a summary of key construct brief definition and key references
is provided in Appendix 7, which offers an overview of the empirical foundations
supporting all the constructs.

2.8 Conceptual Model
Figure 3 presents a conceptual mapping of the relationships among brands, streamers,
and followers in live streaming commerce. It is informed by existing literature yet also
reflects the researcher’s analytical interpretation of how key psychological
mechanisms, including sense of presence, brand–streamer–follower congruence, and
dynamic brand experience, interact within this triadic environment. The model
integrates insights from prior studies with the researcher’s conceptual judgement to
illustrate the broader structure of influences that frame the development of the
proposed conceptual model.

103

Figure 3: Conceptual Summary of Brand–Streamer–Follower Relationships in Live Streaming Commerce

104

In the intricate landscape of live streaming commerce, some literature has consistently
underscored the important role played by streamers in shaping follower engagement
and impulse buying behaviour (Zuo & Xiao, 2021; Fu & Hsu, 2023; Zhang, Cheng &
Huang, 2023; Luo et al., 2024) . The emergent research draws attention to the multi -
layered nature of this influence, dissecting the components that make up the
streamers’ attributes (Yan et al., 2022; He, Li & Xue, 2022; Shao, 2023; Liu et al., 2023;
Zhou & Huang, 2023; Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024)  and the ensuing dynamic brand
experience (Wang et al., 2022; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 2023; Sarker & Dwivedi,
2024). Through a synthesis of the literature, it has become evident that attributes such
as credibility, authenticity, and relatability are not just peripheral qualities but central
pillars in the architecture of consumer engagement within live streaming platfo rms
(Zuo & Xiao, 2021; Fu & Hsu, 2023; Zhang, Cheng & Huang, 2023; Luo et al., 2024).

In addition , the construct of streamer s’ attributes has been widely examined, with
scholars delineating its profound impact on consumer trust and the propensity for
impulse buying. For example, trust is engendered through the streamer's perceived
expertise and honesty, fostering a secure environment that emboldens consumers to
make spontaneous purchases (Yan et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023; Miin et al., 2023; Sun
& Bao, 2023; Luo et al., 2024; Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024) . Also, Authenticity, another
example of the streamer attribute, has been explored extensively, revealing its unique
position in enhancing consumer engagement  (Deng, Lin & Jiang, 2023; Fu & Hsu,
2023; Shao, 2023; Zhang, Cheng & Huang, 2023; Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024) . Authentic
streamers—those who share genuine content, express sincere emotions, and reveal
personal experiences —create an environment where followers can form parasocial
relationships. These relationships, characteri sed by a one -sided emotional bond

105

between the streamer and the follower, have been shown to significantly affect impulse
buying decisions (Lo et al., 2022; Li, Dou & Yu, 2023; Liu et al., 2023; Ma, Aw & Filieri,
2023; Sun & Bao, 2023).

In the realm of live streaming, the sense of presence  and the feeling of immediacy
contribute substantially to the dynamic brand experience  (Ming et al., 2021; Chin et
al., 2023; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 202 3; Pham et al., 2023) . Some literature has
identified the importance of visually appealing content, high -quality audio, and
narrative engagement in captivating the audience's senses and emotions  (Liu, Lei &
Law, 2022; Pham et al., 2023).

Dynamic brand experience also plays an important role in the research. For example,
the cognitive aspect of the dynamic brand experience has also been scrutini sed.
Followers engage in a cognitive process where they assimilate and process
information presented by streamers  (Chen, Chen & Keng, 2020; Wang et al., 2022;
Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023; Li et al., 2023) . This process influences their attitudes
towards the brand and their understanding of the product, ultimately affecting their
impulse buying behaviour (Xu, Huang & Shang, 2021; Dhaigude & Mohan, 2022; Liu
et al., 2023; Khoi, Le & Nguyen Dong, 202 3; Khoi & Le, 2023). Streamers who could
effectively communicate the brand's values and articulate the benefits of a product
contribute to an informed follower base that is more likely to engage in impulse buying
(Fletcher & Gbadamosi, 2022; Liu et al., 202 3; Salem & Alanadoly, 2023; Sarker &
Dwivedi, 2024). Overall, this conceptual model integrates streamer attributes, sense
of presence, congruence, and dynamic brand experience to explain how these

106

interconnected factors collectively shape follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour within live streaming commerce.

2.9 Summary
This chapter critically reviewed existing literature on live streaming commerce,
focusing on how streamers’ attributes —such as credibility, attractiveness, and
expertise—influence dynamic brand experience, follower engagement, and impulse
buying. It also examined the psychological and contextual mechanisms, including the
sense of presence and brand –streamer–follower congruence, that shape co nsumer
responses in live streaming commerce.

The review identified that while existing research recognises the impact of streamer
attributes on consumer trust and engagement (Guo, Zhang & Wang, 2022; Law et al.,
2023; Yun et al., 2023; Zheng et al., 2023) , and streamers who are perceived as
genuine and trustworthy can induce stronger impulse buying behaviours (Xu, Huang
