managing the responses of the questionnaire participants (Field, 2020). After coding
and cleaning the data, the data is imported it into Smart PLS. Then , the data is
computed using descriptive statistics to provide a snapshot of the overall trends, as
well as conducted inferential statistical tests are conducted to uncover any significant
relationships or effects within the data. This  helped the researcher ascertain broad
patterns and understand the general impact of live streaming commerce. Here are the
steps for processing quantitative data. First, the different variables  are dealt with and
given a clear name. Secondly, each variable was worked out with a coding schem e.
Thirdly, all data is imported into SPSS and Smart PLS for analysis. Fourthly, the data
is double-checked make sure all the data to prevent any data errors. By leveraging the
capabilities of SPSS and SMART PLS, the research methods approach provide s a
comprehensive analysis of live streaming commerce.

3.10 Ethical Issues
Ethical issues are critical considerations in research that involve the responsible and
ethical treatment of participants, data, and the overall research process  (Saunders,
Lewis & Thornhill, 2019) .  This research abided by principles of research ethics to
ensure the privacy of participants, as well as the integrity and validity of the research
findings.  Ethical issues of this research encompass several key aspects : obtaining

158

informed consent, maintaining confidentiality, mitigating potential risks, ensuring
fairness, and securing institutional ethical approval.

Prior to participation, all respondents were provided with an informed consent form,
which clearly outlined the study’s purpose, objectives, potential risks, and benefits.
Participants were informed that their involvement was entirely voluntary, and they had
the right to withdraw at any time without providing a reason. The research also
guaranteed that no personal or sensitive information would be collected that could
identify participants.

To maintain confidentiality and data security, all questionnaire responses were
anonymised, ensuring that individual participants could not be traced back to their
responses. Data was securely stored on the OneDrive of the University of Essex, and
access was strictly limited. In compliance with data protection regulations. All collected
data is retained before eventual deletion in line with institutional and ethical guidelines.

Furthermore, this research received ethical approval from the University of Essex
Ethics Committee before data collection commenced (ETH2324 -0555). The ethical
review process ensured that the study complied with institutional guidelines,
safeguarding partic ipant rights and research integrity. By implementing these
measures, this study adheres to high ethical standards, ensuring the credibility,
fairness, and protection of all individuals involved.

159

3.11 Summary
This chapter has outlined the research methodology applied to investigate follower
engagement and  impulse buying  in live streaming commerce, detailing the
philosophical stance, research design, data collection, and data analysis methods. A
pragmatist research philosophy underpins the study, allowing for flexibility in
understanding the complex interactions between streamers, follower engagement and
impulse buying. The quantitative research approach was adopted, with a
questionnaire across China and UK. The questionnaire utilised a 7-point Likert scale
to assess key constructs,  all of them were drawn from validated scales from prior
research.

Besides, data was collected via the online questionnaire platform Qualtrics, using
purposive sampling to target social media live streaming users who actively engage
with streamers through live streaming. To ensure data protection, ethical approval was
obtained from the University of Essex Ethics Committee, wit h informed consent
collected from all participants and data securely stored on the university’s OneDrive,
compliant with regulations. The data was analysed using SPSS and Smart PLS,
employing statistical techniques such as reliability testing, exploratory factor analysis,
and structural equation modelling (SEM) to assess relationships between key
variables. By integrating these methodological choices, this research provides a
structured and robust approach to examining the effectiveness of live streaming
commerce, ensuring the validity, reliability, and generali sability of the findings.  The
next chapter conducted data analysis based on these research methods.

160

4 Chapter 5: Data Analysis and Results
This chapter presents the empirical results derived from the analysis of data collected
via online questionnaire conducted using Qualtrics. The questionnaire encompassed
responses from 600 individuals, evenly divided between 300 Chinese and 300 UK
respondents, to uncover the live streaming commerce across different cultural
contexts. This data  analysis is grounded in a live streaming commerce framework
utilising both SPSS and the Smart PLS to explore the different relationships.

The objective of this research is to provide a thorough empirical understanding of how
specific attributes of streamers and the dynamic brand experiences influence follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviours within the live streaming commerce
environment. This chapter not only tests the hypotheses but also uncover s the
underlying mechanisms through different cultures.

To achieve this, a series of statistical analyses w as performed. Initially, descriptive
statistics provided a preliminary overview of the data, offering insights into the basic
characteristics and normative trends of our sample. This was crucial for ensuring the
data’s integrity and suitability for further a nalysis. Following this, reliability testing,
specifically Cronbach’s alpha, was used to assess the internal consistency of the
questionnaire instruments, ensuring that the scales used to measure various
constructs were both reliable and valid for our cross -cultural examination (Brannen,
2016; Bell, 2019; Hair et al., 2019b; Field, 2020).

Further, exploratory factor analysis was conducted to verify the factor structure of the
questionnaire questions, aiding in the validation of the constructs and ensuring that

161

the data collection methods effectively captured the intended variables. This step was
critical in refining the theoretical constructs based on empirical evidence, thus
enhancing the rigour of the research.

The confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) is used to test whether the data fit a predefined
measurement model. Also, CFA was used to confirm the validity and reliability of the
measurement model, while patterns or relationships between variables are better
assessed through techniques such as SEM. This analysis served as a foundation for
the subsequent advanced statistical procedures, guiding the formulation of more
specific hypotheses about the relationships between streamer attributes, sense of
presence, and viewer responses.

The core of the analytical approach involved Exploratory Factor Analysis and
Confirmatory Factor Analysis. Those analyses were crucial for determining the impact
of independent variables, such as streamer credibility, attractiveness, and expertise ,
on dependent outcomes such as follower engagement and impulse buying. These
analyses tested the direct and indirect effects posited in the hypotheses, providing a
clear picture of how various elements of live streaming influence follower engagement
and impulse buying behaviour.

Also in this research, SmartPLS was selected as the analytical tool for several
important reasons, particularly its suitability for the context and research design of this
investigation. SmartPLS is a variance -based structural equation modelling (SEM)
technique that is particularly well -suited for exploratory research, making it ideal for
this research, which seeks to investigate relationships between streamers’ attributes,

162

sense of presence, congruence, dynamic brand experience, and follower engagement
and impulse buying  behaviour in the emerging field of live streaming commerce.
Another reason for choosing SmartPLS over covariance -based SEM is its ability to
handle small to medium sample sizes without compromising the robustness of the
results (Peng & Lai, 2012; Hair et al., 2019) . Given the cross -cultural nature of this
research, which involves participants from the United Kingdom and China, SmartPLS
is particularly advantageous as it allows for the simultaneous analysis of complex
models across groups, making it suitable for assessing potential cultural differences
and ensuring measurement invariance across contexts.  However, while SmartPLS
offers flexibility and practical advantages, its robustness can be affected by model
complexity and data quality, so results should be interpreted with these limitations in
mind.

Based on the results of SMART PLS in presenting these findings, the chapter
discusses significant relationships and trends observed in the data, with a particular
focus on comparative analysis between Chinese and the United Kingdom samples.
This comparative approach is designed to highlight cultural differences in follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviour and perceptions related to live streaming,
providing a deeper understanding of global digital marketing strategies. The following
sections of this chapter present a detailed analysis of the research findings.

4.1 Data Preparation and Coding
Data acquired from the Qualtrics online questionnaire were imported into SPSS for
initial evaluation and analysis. During this phase, crucial steps were taken to structure
and organise the dataset effectively. This involved assigning descriptive names and

163

appropriate labels to each variable, which facilitated easier identification and
manipulation within the analysis. Additionally, the scale types relevant to each variable
were carefully defined in SPSS, ensuring that the data adhered to the correct
measurement levels, such as nominal, ordinal, interval, or ratio. This meticulous
process of data coding is fundamental to preparing the dataset for the comprehensive
statistical analysis that follows.

4.2 Initial Data Review
Before proceeding with the main statistical evaluation, the collected data underwent a
thorough initial assessment to ensure its readiness for deeper analysis. This process
comprised several fundamental steps: a) an examination of any missing data to
understand the extent and pattern of data absence; b) the identification and analysis
of outliers to ascertain their impact on the overall dataset; and c) the validation of
prerequisites for multivariate analysis, adhering to the methodologies recommended
by Hair, Howard & Nitzl (2020). This preparatory review is crucial to establish ing a
solid foundation for the comprehensive statistical analysis that follows, ensuring that
the data is robust and reliable for advanced statistical techniques (Dawson, 2016;
Flick, 2018).

4.2.1 Missing Data
Missing data represent instances where the values for one or more variables are
unavailable, creating potential obstacles for comprehensive data analysis. Such gaps
could significantly undermine the validity and generalizability of research findings. It is
thus crucial for researchers to promptly identify and address these issues to preserve
the reliability of their study outcomes (Hair et al., 2019). In response to this challenge,

164

Hair et al (2019) have developed a detailed four-step procedure: 1. Ensure your data
is coded correctly. 2. Identify missing values within each variable. 3. Look for patterns
of missingness. 4. Check for associations between missing and observed data. These
four-step procedures were followed in this research, and no missing data points were
noted.

First, all data is encoded. Second, the dataset was examined for missing values using
descriptive statistics and visual inspections to identify any incomplete responses.
Third, the extent and pattern of missing data were analysed to determine whether the
data were missing completely at random (MCAR), at random (MAR), or not at random
(NMAR), using tests such as Little’s MCAR test. Then, appropriate strategies, such as
listwise deletion, mean or median imputation, multiple imputation, or maximum
likelihood e stimation, would have been employed if missing data were detected.
Fourth, a data validation step confirmed the completeness and integrity of the dataset,
ensuring it was suitable for subsequent analysis.

The result shows no missing data points were identified in this research, eliminating
the need for corrective measures and ensuring the robustness of the dataset for
statistical analysis using SmartPLS.

4.2.2 Outlier Detection
Outlier detection is an essential step in quantitative data analysis, particularly in
behavioural research where individual responses may disproportionately influence
regression outcomes. This study employed Cook’s Distance, a well -established
diagnostic tool in multiple regression analysis, to identify potential multivariate outliers

165

within the UK dataset. Cook’s Distance is especially useful for evaluating whether
individual cases have an undue influence on the estimation of regression coefficients,
which can compromise the validity of statistical inferences (Nurunnabi & West 2012 ;
Kyriazos & Poga 2023).

Figure 6 below illustrates the distribution of Cook’s Distance values for all 300 UK
participants across the full range of variables included in this study. These variables
represent the theoretical model and include Streamer’s Attributes, Sense of Presence,
Brand–Streamer–Follower Congruence, Dynamic Brand Experiences, Follower
Engagement, and Impulse Buying.

Figure 6: Outlier Detection of UK Participants

Each participant’s data was included simultaneously in a comprehensive regression
model using SPSS, and Cook’s Distance values were computed to measure the
influence of each case on the model’s predictions. The y-axis of Figure 6 plots Cook’s

166

Distance values, while the x-axis shows recoded Likert-scale responses ranging from
-3 (strongly disagree) to +3 (strongly agree), providing a visual spread of participant
agreement levels across all items. Each dot represents one observation (participant),
allowing a visual inspection of how much any single case could affect the model if
removed.

Cook’s Distance quantifies the influence of each observation on the regression
coefficients. Larger values indicate cases that, if removed, would cause substantial
changes in the regression estimates. According to conventional cut -offs, any Cook’s
Distance value exceeding 1.0 suggests a potentially influential outlier (Cohen et al.,
2013).

The vertical axis in Figure 6 shows Cook’s Distance values, ranging approximately
from 0.00 to 0.04. These values indicate the extent to which individual participants
may influence the model’s regression estimates. The x axis displays recoded Likert-
scale responses ranging.

The majority of  data points in the plot cluster tightly at the lower end of the vertical
axis, with Cook’s Distance values between 0.00 and 0.02, suggesting that most UK
participants have minimal influence on the model. A smaller subset of points rises
toward 0.03, and a few approach 0.04, indicating a slightly higher, but still modest,
level of influence. Importantly, none of the values exceed the conventional threshold
of 1.0, which is widely accepted as the cut -off point for identifying potentially
problematic observations (Cohen et al., 2013). Therefore, no individual case within the

167

UK sample can be considered an extreme outlier or a highly influential point in this
context.

In addition , the y-axis shows  the spread of the data points across the x -axis that
appears reasonably balanced and symmetrical, with no apparent clustering at the
extremes. This distribution implies a consistent model fit across participants and
supports the robustness of the regression estimation. No single participant
disproportionately skews the output or drives the findings (Cohen et al., 2013).

Outlier detection is a critical diagnostic step in regression modelling, as it allows
researchers to evaluate whether any individual data point exerts a disproportionate
influence on the estimated regression coefficients. For the China’s sample, Cook’s
Distance was used as the primary diagnostic measure. This method evaluates both
the leverage (the extent to which an observation’s values differ from the mean of the
predictors) and the residual (the difference between observed and predicted  values),
making it an effective tool for identifying influential observations in a multivariate
context (Nurunnabi & West 2012; Kyriazos & Poga 2023).

Figure 7 below presents the Cook’s Distance plot generated from the regression
analysis involving all variables from the China dataset. These variables include all key
constructs examined in this study: Streamer’s Attributes, Sense of Presence, Brand–
Streamer–Follower Congruence, Dynamic Brand Experiences, Follower Engagement,
Impulse Buying

168

Figure 7: Outlier Detection of Chinese Participants

In Figure 7 above, the y -axis shows Cook’s Distance values ranging from
approximately 0.00 to 0.06, and the x -axis plots the displays recoded Likert -scale
responses ranging. Each dot represents an individual respondent. The vast majority
of points fall below 0.03, indicating minimal influence on the model. A few observations
approach 0.06, but none exceed the critical threshold of 1.0, confirming that no cases
exert an undue influence on the regression results (Cohen et al., 2013).

It is also important to clarify that this scatterplot reflects the influence of all measured
constructs collectively—not just individual items or Likert scale categories. The use of
Cook’s Distance in this context is appropriate for identifying multivariat e outliers in
complex behavioural models, particularly those examining constructs like impulse
buying and engagement in cross-cultural behaviour.

169

This analysis confirms that the Chinese dataset is free from influential multivariate
outliers, and the regression model’s estimates can be considered robust and reliable.

Taken together, the outlier analysis for the United Kingdom  and China dataset
confirms the reliability and integrity of the data used for regression modelling. By
incorporating all variables and analysing the full sample simultaneously, this diagnostic
procedure reinforces the stability of the model’s estimates. As a result, the conclusions
derived from the UK  and China portion of this study can be regarded as statistically
defensible and free from undue influence by individual cases.

4.4 Descriptive Statistics
4.4.1 Normality
According to Hair et al (2019) the reliability of multivariate analysis techniques largely
depends on successfully meeting foundational assumptions: normality, ensuring data
follows a Gaussian distribution. To uphold the validity of the statistical methods
employed in this research, comprehensive tests to verify these assumptions were
executed for each individual construct involved in the research.
Normality in statistical analysis refers to the extent to which the data distribution of
each variable under study conforms to a theoretical normal distribution  (Hair et al

174

2019). The concept of non -normality is evaluated based on two critical factors: the
geometric shape of the data distribution and the overall sample size according to Hair
et al (2019) . Specifically, the shape of a distribution is determined by calculating its
kurtosis and skewness metrics. In an ideal scenario for a normal distribution, both the
kurtosis and skewness values are expected to be 0, indicating perfect symmetry.
Values diverging from 0 signify non-normality, indicating the presence of a skewed or
excessively peaked distribution (Hair et al., 2019). Also, small deviations from 0 do not
necessarily indicate serious non -normality, especially in larger samples. It may also
be helpful to note the thresholds often cited in the literature (e.g. ±1 or ±2) for
determining acceptable levels of skewness and kurtosis in practice(Hair et al., 2019).

Moreover, to rigorously test for deviations from normality, statistical tests such as the
Kolmogorov-Smirnov test (commonly referred to as the KS test) are utili sed (Hair et
al., 2019). This particular test is designed to evaluate the hypothesis that an observed
distribution significantly differs from a normal distribution. Thus, when the results of
the KS test are statistically significant, they point to a substantial variation from
normality, confirming the data's non-adherence to the normal distribution model (Hair
et al., 2019) . This thorough approach helps in understanding the extent of non -
normality and guides their analytical strategies accordingly.

In this investigation, histograms were constructed for each variable using SPSS, which
illustrated deviations from the zero benchmark typically expected for kurtosis and
skewness measures. Despite these deviations, the negative impact of non -normality
can be substantially minimi sed in the presence of a large sample size. As noted by
Hair et al (2019), for sample sizes exceeding 200, the repercussions of non-normality

175

are often negligible. With a sample size of 600 consists of two distinct sub-groups UK
and China, 300 each in  this research, the effects of non -normality are considered to
be minimal. The Kolmogorov-Smirnov test produced significant results, prompting the
need for additional investigations. Descriptive statistics in both countries indicated both
positive and negative values for skewness and kurtosis across different variables,
though these values remained below the critical thresholds proposed by Bao ( 2013)
and Hair et al (2019). An exhaustive exploration of the descriptive statistics post-factor
analysis is thoroughly documented in section 5.5, descriptive analysis of the research,
providing a deep dive into the underlying data characteristics and their implications for
the research findings.

Table 2: Descriptive Data Streamer’s Attributes UK & China

Across both datasets, the mean scores for all measurement items fall within a narrow
range, generally above 4 on a 5 -point Likert scale. This suggests a consistently
favourable perception of streamer attributes among participants. For example, in the
UK sample, item means range from 4.20 (ATTR_2) to 4.65 (TRUS_4), while in the
Chinese sample, the corresponding range spans from 3.95 (TRUS_3) to 4.40
(ATTR_4). This pattern reflects a broadly positive evaluation of streamers in both
markets, albeit with slightly lower means observed in the Chinese context.

Standard deviations are also relatively consistent within and between the two samples.
The UK sample shows standard deviations ranging from 1.405 to 1.629, while the
China sample ranges from 1.510 to 1.655. These values indicate moderate variability
around the mean, implying that while overall perceptions were positive, individual
evaluations still exhibited a degree of dispersion —suitable for further multivariate
analysis (MacGillivray, 1986; Bao, 2013).

To assess the assumption of univariate normality, skewness and kurtosis values were
examined. Skewness evaluates the symmetry of the data distribution. In the UK
sample, skewness values range from -0.396 (TRUS_3) to -0.195 (EXPE_1), and in
the China sample from -0.236 (ATTR_3) to 0.152 (TRUS_3). All values fall within the
accepted threshold of ±2.7 (MacGillivray, 1986; Bao, 2013), suggesting that the data

178

distributions are sufficiently symmetric to support parametric statistical techniques
(Kozłowski & Gawelczyk, 2002).

Kurtosis, which reflects the ‘tailedness’ or peakedness of a distribution, was similarly
within acceptable limits. UK items ranged from -0.781 to -0.370, while Chinese items
ranged from -0.889 to -0.578. These values are well below the recommended
threshold of 5.0 for acceptable kurtosis  (Negarestani et al., 2019; Chen & Genton,
2023).

Low to moderate kurtosis indicates that the data do not display extreme outliers or
flatness that would compromise the validity of statistical inference  outliers (Ruppert,
1987; Balanda & Macgillivray, 1990).

It is important to note that while formal normality tests such as the Kolmogorov –
Smirnov or Shapiro –Wilk tests were not applied, skewness and kurtosis provide
sufficient effect-size-based evidence of normality, especially given the large sample
size (N = 600 total). In large samples, even minor deviations from normality can lead
to statistically significant results in formal normality tests, which is why skewness and
kurtosis are preferred for assessing distributional shape in this context (Negarestani
et al., 2019; Chen & Genton, 2023).

In conclusion, the descriptive statistics confirm that the dataset is suitable for
subsequent analyses such as structural equation modelling. Both central tendency
and dispersion measures reflect coherent and valid patterns across samples, while

179

the skewness and kurtosis statistics support the assumption of univariate normality
required for PLS-SEM.

their inclusion in subsequent structural model testing.

4.4.3 Normality
Assessing the normality of the dataset is a critical step in determining the suitability of
subsequent statistical analyses, particularly when deciding between parametric and
non-parametric techniques (Hair et al., 2019) . Although Partial Least Squares
Structural Equation Modelling (PLS -SEM)—the primary analysis technique used in
this study—does not assume multivariate normality, a detailed normality test is still
warranted to provide a comprehensive understanding of the data’s distribution
characteristics and to support robustness checks. This study employed the
Kolmogorov–Smirnov (K –S) test, a non -parametric method widely used to assess
whether a sample deviates from a standard normal distribution(Bao, 2013).

The Normality Results for UK Sample first part of Table 8 above displays the results
of the Kolmogorov–Smirnov test for the UK sample (N = 300). This includes observed
values for the test statistic (Sta) and the significance level (Sig.) for each indicator
across all key constructs, such as:
 • Streamer’s Attributes (ATTR_1–4)
 • Trustworthiness (TRUS_1–5)
 • Expertise (EXPE_1–4)
 • Telepresence (TELE_1–4)
 • Congruence dimensions (Self, Brand, Follower)
 • Dynamic Brand Experience dimensions (Behaviour, Interaction,
Sensory, Cognition, Reflection)
 • Follower Engagement (FE_1–6)
 • Impulse Buying (IB_1–4)

All K –S test statistics for the UK range between 0.129 and 0.182, with p -values
consistently reported as less than 0.001 (p < 0.001). This indicates that for each item,
the null hypothesis of normality is rejected at a 99.9% confidence level. These
outcomes confirm that the data for each construct significantly deviate from a normal
distribution ( Bao, 2013, Hair et al., 2019).

This pattern of non -normality is consistent with expectations in consumer behaviour
research using Likert-scale instruments, which often yield ordinal, skewed data. While
traditionally such deviations might warrant transformation or caution in applying
parametric methods, the PLS-SEM approach is known for its tolerance of non-normal
data, making it well-suited to this study. Furthermore, the use of bootstrapping in PLS-

197

SEM helps to generate reliable estimates and standard errors despite the non-normal
distribution of input variables.

Normality Results for China Sample, the second half of Table 8 above presents the
results of the Kolmogorov–Smirnov test for the China sample (N = 300), covering the
same constructs and measurement items. The observed test statistics for the Chinese
dataset fall within a similar range of 0.115 to 0.159, again with all p-values reported as
p < 0.001. This demonstrates that the data collected from Chinese participants also
significantly deviates from the normal distribution, and the null hypothesis of normality
is rejected for all items( Bao, 2013, Hair et al., 2019).

This finding is not unusual in cross -cultural questionnaire research, where cultural
response patterns, such as central tendency bias or extreme responding, may further
influence the normality of distribution. Nonetheless, the robustness of PLS -SEM in
handling non-normal data ensures that this characteristic does not adversely affect the
quality or interpretability of the structural model results.

The consistent rejection of normality in both the UK and China datasets underscores
the importance of selecting an analytical approach that is robust to distributional
assumptions. In this study, the decision to adopt PLS -SEM is reinforced by these
results, as the method does not rely on multivariate normality and is particularly
effective for complex models with multiple latent constructs and relatively small -to-
moderate sample sizes.

198

In conclusion, the Kolmogorov –Smirnov test results presented in Table 8 above
confirm that the data do not conform to the assumptions of normality. However, this
characteristic does not compromise the study’s methodological rigour, given the
strengths of the PLS -SEM framework employed. These findings also reflect broader
patterns o bserved in behavioural and marketing research using subjective
assessments measured via Likert-type items.

4.5 Confirmatory Factor Analysis
Exploratory Factor Analysis assists researchers in determining the appropriate
number of factors needed to represent collected data by analy sing factor loadings. In
contrast, Confirmatory Factor Analysis differs from Exploratory Factor Analysis
because it requires the researcher to specify both the number of factors and the
specific items that load onto each factor based on theoretical framewor ks (Hair et al.,
2019). Confirmatory Factor Analysis enables the validation or rejection of a theory by
assessing how well the theoretically defined factors align with the actual data.
Therefore, Confirmatory Factor Analysis is grounded in measurement theory, where
predefined factors or conceptual constructs are incorporated into the measurement
model (Hair et al., 2019) . According Hair et al (2019) , the process of conducting
Exploratory Factor Analysis involves several steps:
• Measurement Model Development: Establishing the initial model based on
theoretical foundations.
• Measurement Model Specification and Identification: Defining the model's
structure and ensuring it is statistically identifiable.
• Model Fit Measurement and Assessment: Evaluating how well the model fits
the collected data using fit indices.

199

• Model Validity Measurement and Assessment: Testing for reliability and validity
to confirm that the model accurately measures the intended constructs.

4.5.1 Factor Loadings
The appropriate factor extraction and rotation methods have been applied ; the
interpretation of the factors relies heavily on distinguishing them based on their factor
loadings. These loadings are essentially the correlation coefficients between each item
and the identified factor, providing a measure of how strongly each item is associated
with a particular factor. Generally, factor loadings that fall within the range of ±.30 to
±.40 are considered adequate for a reliable interpretation of the factor structure.
However, loadings that reach or exceed ±.50 are regarded as having practical
significance, indicating a strong relationship between the factor and the item
(Peterson, 2000; Sass, 2010).

Furthermore, the significance of these factor loadings can also be influenced by the
sample size utili sed in the study, as noted by Pallant (2020) . According to their
guidelines, a factor loading of .30 is significant when the sample size is at least from
3 to 20 times the number of variables (Mundfrom, Shaw & Ke, 2005). This criterion is
relevant to this study, which has a sample size of 600, closely aligning with the
threshold suggested by Pallant (2020). Despite this guideline, the current study opts
for a higher threshold, considering factor loadings of .40 as the benchmark for
significance in the context of exploratory factor analysis. This decision is based on the
specific analytical goals and the nature of the data under examination, emphasising a
more stringent criterion for defining significant relationships within the factor analysis
framework Pallant (2020).

200

The factor loadings are shown below (Table 9), to summarise, in terms of outcomes,
6 factors were extracted, guided by eigenvalues greater than 1.0 and visual inspection
of the scree plot. These factors collectively explained 70.6% in China  and 75.75% in
the UK of the total variance, indicating a satisfactory level of explanatory power. In
addition, all items reached the minimum level of factor loading value of 0.4, so no items
were removed.

The final factor solution displayed clean and interpretable loading structures, with each
retained item strongly loading onto its intended construct. For example, items ATTR_1
to ATTR_5 loaded onto the Streamer's Attributes factor with loadings ranging fro m
.671 to .974 in the UK and .664 to .751 in China. While items TELE_1 to TELE_4 and
SC_1 to SC_4 were loaded onto the Sense of Presence factor. These results confirm
that the constructs are reliably measured and that the instrument captures the
intended theoretical dimensions with acceptable psychometric properties.  This
rigorous refinement process through factor analysis thus laid a solid foundation for the
subsequent stages of the analysis, ensuring the robustness and validity of the
constructs used in the structural model.

4.5.2 Cronbach's Alpha Reliability Analysis
Reliability analysis is an essential component of research that assesses the
consistency of a measurement instrument. Reliability analysis is a crucial statistical
process used to measure the consistency and stability of a measurement instrument
over time. One of the most common methods for evaluating reliability is the calculation
of Cronbach’s alpha (George & Mallery, 2019) . Cronbach's alpha is a statistic
commonly used to measure the internal consistency, or reliability, of a set of scale or
test items. Introduced by Cronbach, (1951), this coefficient is a measure of the extent
to which all parts or items of a test contribute positively and consistently to the overall
test score. Specifically, it assesses how closely related a set of items are as a group
(Cronbach, 1951).  According to Sijtsma & Pfadt, (2021), if measurements or variables
are "parallel," they exhibit equal covariances and variances. In cases where
measurements are "essentially tau-equivalent," they do not share equal variances, but
their covariances remain consistent (Graham, 2006). Consequently, Sijtsma & Pfadt,
(2021) notes that Cronbach’s alpha effectively gauges the reliability of either the sum
or average of measurements that meet these parallel or less restrictive essentially tau-
equivalent conditions. On the other hand, if the measurements are "congeneric" as
described, they can be modelled using a single factor with uncorrelated measurement
errors. The congeneric assumption is considered more realistic than tau -equivalence
because it does not require equal variances or covariances among the measurements
(Cho, 2021). The internal consistency of a measurement tool is gauged through the
correlations among various items that comprise the same test. Such correlations
signify that if multiple items are designed to assess the same underlying construct,
they ought to generate comparable scores. To measure this consistency, Cronbach’s
alpha is utilised, which involves calculating the correlation across all possible pairs of

204

items within the test. The resulting measure of internal consistency can vary widely,
ranging from zero to one, with instances where negative values are observed as well.

This metric is crucial for validating the reliability and coherence of the measurement
instruments used in psychological and educational assessments. Psychometric
testing, it is generally recognised that the value of Cronbach's alpha serves as a
benchmark for assessing the reliability of an instrument. Specifically, a Cronbach's
alpha ranging from 0.6 to 0.7 is regarded as indicating an acceptable level of reliability
for the tool being evaluated. Values that reach 0.8 or higher are typically viewed as
indicative of an excellent  reliability standard. Conversely, a Cronbach's alpha
exceeding 0.95, although seemingly high, is not always desirable as it may suggest
that there is redundancy among the items, which could mean some items are
superfluous. This potential issue of redundancy highlights the complexity of designing
effective measurement tools (Ursachi, Horodnic & Zait, 2015).

While it would be ideal for all items within a test to uniformly measure the same
underlying latent variable, there are numerous instances where Cronbach's alpha
might yield higher values, even when the items are actually assessing disparate
constructs. Th is phenomenon highlights some limitations of relying solely on
Cronbach's alpha as an indicator of internal consistency (Raykov, 2004; Hayes &
Coutts, 2020). In a comprehensive evaluation involving the analysis of hundreds of
research articles and several thousand coefficients extracted from them, Peterson,
(1994) found that the average value of Cronbach's alpha across various studies is
approximately 0.77. This statistic underscores the typical reliability level encountered

205

in empirical research, reflecting the commonality of the measure but also hinting at the
diversity of contexts in which it is applied.

Ursachi, Horodnic & Zait, (2015) offer a widely accepted set of benchmarks. Therefore,
targeting a Cronbach's alpha score of approximately 0.8 is often seen as a practical
and reasonable objective. Additionally, while a higher Cronbach's alpha indicates a
strong internal consistency amon g the items of the scale, it does not guarantee the
scale's one-dimensionality.

Table 10 below shows the results of the reliability test. The reliability test using
Cronbach’s alpha is used. In this research, there are 6 main variables with a total of
67 items measured, including Streamer’s Attributes 13 items, Sense of Presence 8
items, Strea mer, Brand, Follower Congruence 19 items, Dynamic Experiences 17
items, Follower Engagement 6 items, and Impulse Buying 4 items and all the variables
were tested in 2 countries UK and China.

207

4.5.3 Measurement Model Specification
To develop a measurement model for Confirmatory Factor Analysis, it is essential to
ensure unidimensionality, especially when dealing with multiple constructs. This
concept means that a set of measured items should align with only one construct (Hair
et al., 2019). Additionally, the constructs need to be categorised as either reflective or
formative. Reflective measurement models assume that latent constructs influence the
observed variables, while formative models suggest that the constructs are defined by
their indicators rather than being latent. Errors in a for mative measurement model
arise from the inability of measured items to fully define the construct (Hair et al.,
2019). In this study, the constructs used for CFA are reflective and latent, leading to a
design where arrows point from the latent construct to the measured items.

4.5.4 Assessment of Measurement Model Validity
Construct validity ensures that the items used in a measurement accurately reflect the
intended latent constructs. It is typically evaluated using two main criteria: convergent
validity and discriminant validity. Convergent validity assesses how well items
correlate with the construct they are meant to represent and is determined by
examining factor loadings, average variance extracted, and construct reliability (Hair
et al., 2019).

High factor loadings indicate strong convergent validity, signifying that the items are
well-aligned with the underlying construct they are meant to measure. As a general
guideline, factor loadings above 0.50 are acceptable, though values of 0.70 or higher
are preferred. In structural equation modeling software like Stata, these loadings are
represented by standardized regression coefficients (Hair et al., 2019).

208

The average variance extracted is another key metric for assessing convergent
validity, representing the mean variance captured by the items relative to the construct.
A value greater than 0.50 indicates satisfactory convergence (Hair et al., 2019).

To further confirm convergent validity, construct reliability is used, with values over
0.70 generally signaling strong reliability. However, construct reliability values
between 0.60 and 0.70 may still be acceptable if other indicators of convergent validity
are strong (Hair et al., 2019).

Discriminant validity, the second aspect of construct validity, confirms that each
construct is distinct from others, ensuring that items measure only one construct. This
is evaluated by ensuring that the AVE of a construct exceeds the squared correlations
with other constructs (Hair et al., 2019).

construct validity. Based on the outcomes of the Exploratory Factor Analysis,

210

subsequently removed to enhance both model fit and construct validity. The initial
model fit was evaluated using absolute fit indices and incremental fit indices. Table 11
presents the fit statistics from the first Confirmatory Factor analysis, indicating that
there is room for improvement in the model's fit indices.

In the UK sample, all 1 4 constructs demonstrated strong reliability, with CR values
ranging from 0.901 to 0.945. The constructs with the highest CR included Streamer –
Follower Congruence (0.945) and Expertise (0.941), while Relative Experiences
(0.901) recorded the lowest , but still well above the acceptable threshold. This
indicates that each construct’s items were consistently measuring the intended
underlying factor. Similarly, in the Chinese sample, CR values ranged from 0.863 to
0.923. The highest reliability was observ ed in the Trustworthiness construct (0.923),
while the lowest was found in Intellectual (0.863). These results confirm that, across
both cultural contexts, the items used to measure each latent construct performed
consistently and reliably.

Regarding AVE, which reflects the proportion of variance captured by a construct
relative to the variance due to measurement error, all constructs across both samples
exceeded the recommended minimum threshold of 0.50. In the UK dataset, AVE
values ranged from 0.682 (Streamer –Follower Congruence) to 0.831 (Behavioural
Experience), indicating satisfactory convergent validity across the board. In the

212

Chinese dataset, AVE values ranged from 0.550 (Streamer–Follower Congruence) to
0.778 (Behavioural Experience). Although the AVE values for some constructs in
China were comparatively lower than those in the UK, they remained above the
acceptable level, confirming adequate convergence.

4.5.7 Discriminant Validity Assessment
Discriminant validity refers to the extent to which constructs that are expected to be
distinct are indeed empirically separate from one another (Hair et al., 2019). In this
study, discriminant validity was assessed using the Heterotrait -Monotrait ratio of
correlations (HTMT), which is considered one of the most reliable and conservative
methods for evaluating construct distinctiveness in PLS -SEM (Henseler, Ringle &
Sarstedt, 2015).

For both the UK and China datasets, HTMT values were compared against the widely
accepted thresholds of 0.85 (Kline, 2011) and 0.90 (Gold, Malhotra & Segars, 2001).
Results for the China sample showed that all HTMT values ranged between 0.19 and
0.49, comfortably below the recommended cut-off. Similarly, results for the UK sample
demonstrated that all HTMT ratios also fell well below 0.85, with values between 0.20
and 0.49.

These findings provide robust evidence of discriminant validity across both cultural
contexts, confirming that each construct —such as streamer attributes, sense of
presence, dynamic brand experiences, follower engagement, and impulse buying —
measures a unique dimension of live streaming commerce.

213

Taken together, the HTMT assessment confirms that the measurement model meets
the criteria for discriminant validity, thereby supporting the adequacy of the construct
operationalisation for subsequent structural model testing.

Overall, the confirmatory Factor Analysis assessments indicate that the measurement
model performs well in both the UK and Chinese samples. The constructs demonstrate
consistent and valid measurement across different cultural settings, lending credibility
to the subsequent structural model analysi s. These results also support the cross -
cultural robustness of the instrument, which is particularly important given the
comparative nature of this study.

4.6 Common Method Bias
In this section, t he way to reduce common bias is discussed to minimise common
method bias . This research implemented both procedural measures during the
research design phase and statistical tests during data analysis. Procedural steps
were carefully planned based on suggestions of Kock, (2017) to reduce the likelihood
of respondents introducing bias into their responses.

First, respondent anonymity was ensured by guaranteeing that all questionnaire
responses would remain confidential and anonymous, which likely reduced social
desirability bias and encouraged honest answers. Second, established scales with
demonstrated validity and reliability were used to measure each construct, ensuring
that items were well-defined and clearly worded to avoid ambiguity. Third, scale clarity
was enhanced by pre -testing the questionnaire instrument with a small group of
participants to refine item wording and structure. Fourth, counterbalancing the order

214

of items in the questionnaire helped to reduce order effects, preventing systematic
bias due to the sequential positioning of questions. Additionally, psychological
separation of constructs was achieved by using distinct question blocks and varying
item phrasing to create conceptual distance between the independent and dependent
variables (MacKenzie & Podsakoff, 2012).

In addition to these procedural measures, statistical tests were employed to assess
the presence of common method bias in the dataset. Specifically, Harman’s single -
factor test was conducted (Aguirre-Urreta & Hu, 2019), and the results confirmed that
no single factor explained the majority of the variance. Furthermore, the marker
variable technique was applied to control for potential bias statistically (Simmering et
al., 2015). Together, these procedural and statistical steps provide robust evidence
that common method bias was effectively minimised, ensuring the validity of the
findings in this study. More detailed analysis process is shown below.

There are several approaches to addressing Common Method Bias, but the most
recognised are the Harman one-factor test and the marker variable method (Lindell &
Whitney, 2001). Both of these methods also be used for testing in this research. Based
on the Harman one-factor test. If a single factor explains most of the variance (more
than 50%), then common method bias may be present (Fuller et al., 2016; Howard,
2023). For this research, the total variance explained by all retained factors in this
research is 25.04% in the UK and 23.23% in China , so data from both countries  are
less than 50%, which indicates that this shows that common method bias is not a major
problem in this study.

215

In the application of the marker variable method, a variable unrelated to the main
variables of interest —Weather—was utilised. This particular variable  was measured
through four specific items on a 7 -point Likert scale, with response options ranging
from Strongly disagree to Strongly agree. Initially, the calculated the average
correlation between the constructs without incorporating the marker variable  is
calculated, resulting in a mean correlation of 0.48 in the UK and  0.43 in China .
Subsequently, the marker variable was integrated into the measurement models, and
inter-construct correlations were recalculated. After this integration, the mean
correlation observed remained at 0.48 in the UK and 0.42 in China. The analysis
revealed that the average difference between the adjusted correlation matrices (which
included the marker variable) and the unadjusted correlation matrices (which did not
include the marker variable) was 0.00 in UK and 0.01 in China. This minimal difference
indicates that the inclusion of the marker variable did not cause any significant
deviation in the correlation matrices. Consequently, these findings suggest that
common method bias is not a significant concern in this study, as the introduc tion of
the marker variable did not materially alter the relationships among the constructs
being examined (Lindell & Whitney, 2001). The consistency between the adjusted and
unadjusted correlation matrices reinforces the conclusion that the marker variable's
influence is negligible, thereby affirming the robustness of the observed correlations
among the primary constructs under investigation.

4.7 Measurement Invariance
Testing for measurement invariance is a critical step when conducting cross -cultural
research, especially when data is collected from different countries, such as the UK
and China. Measurement invariance ensures that the constructs being measured are

216

interpreted, understood, and function similarly across diverse groups (Jeong & Lee,
2019; Lacko et al., 2022) . This process confirms that observed differences between
groups reflect genuine variations in the constructs , also  in how the measurement
scales operate across cultures.

To provide further clarity, it is important to distinguish between the two complementary,
but distinct procedures used in this study: the Measurement Invariance of Composite
Models (MICOM) procedure and Multi-Group Analysis (MGA). The MICOM procedure,
as o utlined by Henseler, Ringle, and Sinkovics (2009), is designed specifically to
assess measurement invariance in PLS -SEM. It involves a three -step process —
configural invariance, compositional invariance, and equality of composite means and
variances—to dete rmine whether constructs are measured equivalently across
groups. This step is essential to ensure that the constructs and their associated items
operate similarly between UK and Chinese respondents. Only after establishing
sufficient measurement invariance through MICOM, it methodologically appropriate to
proceed with MGA. The Multi -Group Analysis then compares the strength and
significance of structural relationships between the two national groups, enabling to
explore whether cultural context moderates the relationships  within the model. By
following both MICOM and MGA sequentially, this study adheres to best practices in
cross-cultural SEM and supports valid cross-national comparisons.

In the context of this research, which examines constructs such as streamers’
attributes, sense of presence, congruence, and dynamic brand experience, impulse
buying and follower engagement ensuring measurement invariance is essential.
Cultural differences between the United Kingdom and China may lead to variations in

217

how respondents perceive and respond to questionnaire items, influenced by
differences in language, cultural norms, and social behaviours. Without establishing
measurement invariance, any observed differences in follower engagement or impulse
buying behaviour might stem from inconsistencies in the measuremen t instrument
rather than true differences in the constructs themselves. This would compromise the
validity of cross-group comparisons and the robustness of the study's conclusions.

By testing for measurement invariance, this research verifies that the constructs and
their associated measurement items function equivalently across the UK and Chinese
samples. This process enables meaningful and valid comparisons between the two
cultural contexts, providing confidence that the observed effects reflect actual
variations in behaviours or attitudes rather than measurement biases. Ensuring
measurement invariance enhances the reliability and credibility of cross -cultural
findings, making them more robust for academic and practical applications (Henseler,
Ringle & Sinkovics, 2009).

Based on the suggestion of Henseler, Ringle & Sinkovics,(2009), this research utilises
multi-group analysis with structural equation modelling (SEM) in Smart PLS to explore
the differences in structural relationship strengths between participants from the
United Kingdom and China. This approach evaluates whether national groups
influence the primary effects or paths within the structural model. The analysis focuses
on the impact of these different national groups on the structural model and three key
variables: sense of presence, brand follower and streamer congruence, and dynamic
brand experiences. The participants from different national groups  were categorised
in the dataset using nominal variables, as depicted in Table 12 below

Table 12 above shows the results of Measurement Invariance testing (MICOM 2) ,
showing the comparison between different groups, often across various constructs. In
this output, the Measurement Invariance results data from MICOM. In general, there
are 12 variables to be tested : Streamer’s Attributes, Sense of Presence, Streamer,
Brand, Follower, Congruence,  Dynamic Brand Experiences, Follower Engagement,
Impulse Buying, Age, Education, Frequency, Gender, Income, and Previous
Experiences. Three indicators were included in the invariant measurement invariance
assessment: Original Correlation and Correlation Permutation mean , and P -Value.
Based on the suggestion that Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt, (2016) if the P-value < 0.05
indicates measurement invariance, meaning that the groups are comparable with
respect to the construct. In addition, in comparing the values of the Original Correlation
and the Correlation Permutation Mean in measurement invariance testing, the
differences between these two values should be minimal for the assumption of
invariance to hold. The general expectation is that these correlations should be close
to each other, as a significant discrepancy might indicate a lack of invariance
(Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt, 2016; Jr et al., 2017).

The results of the multiple analyses are shown in Table 12 above, where there are 5
cells with the pathway on the left, the path coefficients for the UK  and China in the
second and third column, the path coefficients difference between the UK and China
in the fourth column , and in the fifth column is the Permutation p value. Overall, the
path coefficients for the UK and China are not very large, ranging from -0.099 to 0.134.
For the P value, it is greater than 0.005 for all pathways , which means that all
constructs achieved invariance, allowing valid cross-country comparisons.

220

4.8 Path Analysis
While various methods are available for path analysis, this study employs Partial Least
Squares Structural Equation Modelling (PLS -SEM) due to its suitability for predictive
research, its robustness with small to medium sample sizes, and its ability to han dle
non-normal data distributions (Shmueli et al., 2019; Anon, 2023). The approach is
particularly well -aligned with the exploratory nature of this study, which begins by
clearly identifying and defining individual constructs — a critical foundation for
developing and evaluating the model’s structural relationships. (Shmueli et al., 2019,
Anon, 2023).

Following the identification phase, a measurement model i s constructed. This model
serves as a scaffold, linking theoretical constructs with their practical, observable
representations. The strength and validity of the indicator–construct relationships are
then systematically evaluated using established criteria such as factor loadings,
composite reliability, and average variance extracted (AVE), ensuring that the
measurement model accurately captures the intended constructs ( Hair Jr et al., 2014;
Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt, 2016).

The validation of the measurement model's accuracy and reliability is an essential
precursor next, the focus shifts to specifying the structural model. This phase involves
the application of the PLS-SEM algorithm via the SmartPLS, which efficiently handles
the model's complexity and helps delineate the causal relationships between
constructs. The final step involves a thorough assessment of the structural model’s
validity to ensure that it accurately reflects the theoretical assumptions and can reliably

221

predict outcomes based on the hypothesi sed relationships (F. Hair Jr et al., 2014;
Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt, 2016).
Through these rigorous analytical procedures—such as assessing indicator reliability,
construct validity, and structural relationships —PLS-SEM via SmartPLS enables the
uncovering of complex data patterns while advancing theoretical insights in their field.

4.8.1  Structural Model - Specification
In specifying the structural model, two types of latent constructs: exogenous
constructs, which function as predictors (similar to independent variables), and
endogenous constructs, which are the predicted outcomes (analogous to dependent
variables) is distinguished These constructs are typically measured using multiple
items and are visually represented in a path diagram, where directional relationships
are illustrated with single -headed arrows pointing from exogenous to endogenous
constructs. This structure captures  the hypothesised causal pathways that form the
foundation of the model (F. Hair Jr et al., 2014).

In this research, a structural model was designed based on theoretical principles
including Source Credibility Theory (Hovland & Weiss, 1951) , Flow Theory (Steuer,
1992) and Congruence Theory (Sirgy, 1985; Aaker, 1999) . The model includes
exogenous constructs such as Streamer’s Attributes, sense of presence, and streamer
brand follower congruence . These exogenous constructs influence other latent
constructs like Sense of Presence, Brand Streamer and Follower Congruence and
dynamic brand experience, Follower Engagement and Impulse Buying. These
constructs are subsequently connected to the endogenou s constructs, including

222

Follower Engagement and Impulse Buying, through single -headed arrows indicating
the direction of influence.

Additionally, constructs such as Product Type and Gender are included as moderating
variables in the model. Product Type is comprised of several binary variables
representing different categories, such as Apparel, Food, Jewellery, Beauty Products,
Digital Products, Bag and Accessories, Home Textile, Electrical Appliances, and
Others. Gender is also considered a moderating variable to examine if significant
differences exist in the relationships between constructs across different genders.

Since the endogenous constructs are not entirely explained by the exogenous
constructs alone, error terms are associated with them to account for the unexplained
variance (F. Hair Jr et al., 2014). This ensures that the model captures the complexity,
and potential external influences not directly measured by the included constructs.

Figure 9 below illustrates the structural models developed without , while Figure 10
below shows the structural model with control variables. The control variables included
in the model are Age, Income, Education, the platform used (such as Instagram, Little
Red Book, Other, Taobao, and TikTok), and Previous Experiences.

It is important to clarify that Figure 10 represents the structural model used for the final
analysis, which was tested using raw item -level scores. This approach was chosen
over aggregated construct scores (as shown in Figure 9) because it allows for a more
granular and accurate estimation of measurement and structural relationships. Using
raw item scores aligns with the strengths of the PLS -SEM method, which prioritises

223

maximising explained variance at the indicator level and is especially suitable for
complex models involving multiple reflective constructs (Hair et al., 2019).

Figure 9: Structural model without control variable

Figure 10: Structural model with control variable

By incorporating these detailed elements and explaining the structure and
relationships within the research model, Gaining better understanding of how various
factors, such as Streamer’s Attributes and dynamic brand experiences, impact key

224

outcomes like follower engagement and Impulse buying. This comprehensive model
facilitates a deeper analysis of the interactions and potential moderating effects,
offering valuable insights for researchers and practitioners in the field.

4.9 Hypothesis Testing
The main  hypotheses of this research examine the relationships among 6 core
variables: streamers’ attributes, sense of presence, brand –streamer–follower
congruence, dynamic brand experience, follower engagement, and impulse buying.
Specifically, it is hypothesised  that streamers’ attributes positively influence dynamic
brand experience, which in turn affects follower engagement and impulse buying.
Sense of presence and brand –streamer–follower congruence is proposed as
mediators in the relationship between streamer attributes and dynamic brand
experience. Additionally, moderation effects (e.g. gender and product type) and
moderated mediation effects are also tested using PLS-SEM.

Furthermore, this section presents the results for all the proposed hypotheses,
encompassing direct effects, mediating effects, moderating effects, and moderated
mediation effects. The analyses were conducted using the PLS Algorithm ,
bootstrapping and permutation multigroup analysis. The results are interpreted based
on the statistical outputs provided by these techniques. This approach ensures that
the conclusions drawn are robust and reliable, enhancing the empirical findings (Hair,
Howard & Nitzl, 2020). The findings presented here are crucial for understanding the
complex interactions and conditional relationships between the studied variables, and
they substantiate the theoretical framework proposed in this research. This thorough

225

examination of each hypothesis underscores the strength and validity of the results
obtained.

hypothesised relationships were supported and found to be positive. Except, the
relationship involving sense of presence in the UK was not statistically significant, and
thus Hypotheses 3 and 7 were not supported in that context.

Table 13 above presents the results of the structural model assessment using
bootstrapping with 5,000 subsamples. Each path is evaluated based on its Beta
coefficient (β), T-value, and P-value. A path is considered statistically significant if p <
0.05, with stronger sup port indicated by p < 0.01 or p < 0.001. The STDEV column
represents the standard error of the bootstrapped estimates, where lower values
indicate greater stability. Among the six hypothesised direct paths:

1. Streamer’s Attributes → Sense of Presence was significant in both countries (β
= 0.244, t = 11.919, p < 0.001) and China (β = 0.238, t = 10.153, p < 0.001).

2. Streamer’s Attributes → Brand Streamer and Follower Congruence showed a
strong positive effect in both countries (β = 0.424, t = 18.089, p < 0.001) and
China (β = 0.264, t = 2.797, p < 0.01).

3. Sense of Presence → Dynamic Brand Experience was supported in China but
not in UK (UK: β = 0.487, t = 3. 447, p > 0.20; China: β = 0.485, t = 2.797, p <
0.01).

4. Brand Streamer and Follower Congruence → Dynamic Brand Experience was
significant in both countries (UK: β = 0.487, t = 7.16, p < 0.001; China β = 0.485,
t = 5.460, p < 0.001).

5. Dynamic Brand Experience → Follower Engagement was supported in both
countries (UK: β = 0.251, t = 7.723, p < 0.001; China: β = 0.170, t = 7.723, p <
0.001).

229

6. Dynamic Brand Experience → Impulse Buying was also significant  in both
countries (UK: β = 0.112, t = 5.817, p < 0.001; China: β = 0.172, t = 8.656, p <
0.001).

All hypothesised paths were statistically significant , except Sense of Presence →
Dynamic Brand Experience in the UK, and in the expected positive direction across
both samples, suggesting strong support for the conceptual model. Although most
results were consistent, minor variations were observed in the magnitude of effects.
For instance, Streamer’s Attributes had a notably stronger effect on Brand-Streamer-
Follower Congruence in the UK, whereas Dynamic Brand Experience more strongly
influenced Impulse Buying in China.

These findings confirm that the proposed relationships are robust and applicable
across both cultural contexts, offering insight into the influence mechanisms of
streamer characteristics and brand experience on consumer engagement and
behaviour.

4.9.2  Mediation Analysis
The mediator is a variable that explains the mechanism or process through which an
independent variable influences a dependent variable. In other words, it acts as an
intermediary that helps to clarify the relationship between the two primary variables by
showing how or why such an effect occurs. The mediator carries the influence of the
independent variable to the dependent variable, suggesting that the independent
variable indirectly affects the dependent variable through the mediator. This concept

230

is crucial for understanding the underlying processes that drive observed relationships
in research (MacKinnon, 2008)

Based on Based on the recommendations of Hayes (2018) and Wang et al. (2022),
this study examined sense of presence, brand –streamer–follower congruence, and
dynamic brand experience as mediators in the relationship between streamers’
attributes and the outcomes of follower engagement and impulse buying.

Based on the recommendations of Hayes, (2018) and Wang et al. (2022) , this study
examined sense of presence, brand –streamer–follower congruence, and dynamic
brand experience as mediators in the relationship between streamers’ attributes and
the outcomes of follower engagement and impulse buying.

This mediation approach was adopted because there have been a number of previous
studies in the live streaming domain that have applied this approach and provided little
evidence, e.g. (Ming et al., 2021; Ma, Zou & Lv, 2022; Deng, Chen & Wu, 2024;
Maghraoui & Khrouf, 2024) . Specifically, within live streaming platforms, the factors
that streamers' attributes had a significant direct impact on the relationship of dynamic
brand experiences were included in the mediation analysis . See the analysis below
for more detail s. So, this research is based on previous evidence to strengthen the
research evidence on sense of presence and brand follower streamer congruence as
mediating variables.

In detail, the structural model assessed all direct and indirect effects of follower
engagement and impulse buying, and the analysis showed that the model fit to the

231

data was adequate for estimating the significance of the indirect effects and the
present study used the non -parametric bootstrap method proposed by (Streukens &
Leroi-Werelds, 2016) with 95% confidence intervals for the 5000 sampling. Table  13
below shows the mediation analysis result.

4.9.3 Indirect (Mediating) Effects
Table 14 below presents the results of the hypothesised indirect (mediating) effects
for both the UK and China samples, based on the PLS-SEM bootstrapping procedure
with 5,000 resamples. The analysis evaluates 6 hypothesised sequential mediation
pathways, each comprising a combination of mediators between the independent and
dependent constructs.

In accordance with established significance thresholds in PLS-SEM (Henseler, Ringle
& Sarstedt, 2016; Jr et al., 2017) , indirect effects were considered statistically
significant when: The p-value is less than 0.05 (p < 0.05), significant
And the T -value exceeds ±1.96, indicating the estimate lies outside the 95%
confidence interval. Furthermore, the sign and magnitude of the indirect effect quantify
the strength and direction of the mediating relationship. A positive indirect effect
indicates that the mediating construct transmits a positive influence from the predictor
to the outcome variable. Conversely, a negative indirect effect would reflect an inverse
mediated relationship. Interpretation should be grounded in both statistical outcomes
and theoretical justification (Verma & Verma, 2024).

233

Pathway 1:  Streamer's Attributes → Sense of Presence → Dynamic Brand
Experience → Follower Engagement

This sequential mediation was statistically significant in China (p = 0.031, T = 2.377)
but not significant in the UK (p = 0.075, T = 1.785), falling slightly short of the 5%
threshold. The positive indirect effects (UK = 0.147; China = 0.033) suggest that  in
both contexts, streamers’ presence contributes to heightened brand experiences,
which in turn positively impact follower engagement—though this chain is statistically
supported only in the Chinese context.

Pathway 2:  Streamer's Attributes → Sense of Presence → Dynamic Brand
Experience → Impulse Buying

Similarly, this pathway was significant only in China (p = 0.021, T = 2.471), while the
UK findings were non-significant (p = 0.139, T = 1.628). The positive coefficients (UK
= 0.019; China = 0.033) suggest that the sense of presence increases impulse buying
via enhanced dynamic brand experiences, but this effect is only statistically confirmed
for Chinese consumers.

Pathway 3:  Streamer's Attributes → Brand –Streamer–Follower Congruence →
Dynamic Brand Experience → Follower Engagement

This indirect effect is statistically significant in both the UK and China, though stronger
in the Chinese sample (UK: p = 0.000, T = 1.881; China: p = 0.000, T = 4.601). The
indirect effects are positive (UK = 0.029; China = 0.088), indicating that when t he

234

streamer, follower, and brand are perceived as aligned, this fosters a more immersive
brand experience, which then enhances follower engagement. The greater effect in
China could reflect stronger identification with streamers in local platforms such as
Douyin.

Pathway 4:  Streamer's Attributes → Brand –Streamer–Follower Congruence →
Dynamic Brand Experience → Impulse Buying

This pathway follows the same mediators as above but leads to impulse buying rather
than engagement. Again, it was significant in China (p = 0.000, T = 4.960), but only
marginally significant in the UK (p = 0.003, T = 1.720). While both effect directions are
positive (UK = 0.019; China = 0.088), only the Chinese pathway satisfies both
statistical thresholds. This reinforces the notion that perceived congruence enhances
spontaneous purchase behaviour more strongly in the Chinese live  streaming
commerce context.

Pathway 5:  Streamer's Attributes → Sense of Presence → Dynamic Brand
Experience

This single-step mediation was significant in both countries, though again more robust
in China (UK: p = 0.053, T = 1.864; China: p = 0.007, T = 2.693). The positive indirect
effects (UK = 0.057; China = 0.080) suggest that streamer presence significantly
enhances the perceived quality and immersion of the brand experience, particularly in
contexts where presence cues are more integrated (e.g., livestream tools in China with
dual-camera, chat overlay, etc.).

235

Pathway 6:  Streamer's Attributes → Brand –Streamer–Follower Congruence →
Dynamic Brand Experience

This final indirect path was highly significant in both the UK and China (p = 0.000 for
both; UK T = 6.686; China T = 6.392), representing the strongest mediating effect in
the model. The large positive coefficients (UK = 0.294; China = 0.213) demonstrate
that the alignment among streamer, follower, and brand powerfully predicts the depth
and richness of brand experience. This finding affirms the central role of congruence
in shaping consumer perceptions and validates its inclusion as a core mediator.

4.10  Summary
To summarise, SPSS and SmartPLS software were used to organise and analyse
data collected from 600 valid questionnaires, comprising 300 participants from the UK
and 300 from China. All data were analysed using quantitative methods. Initially, the
dataset w as screened to ensure the absence of missing or invalid responses.
Following this, descriptive statistics and exploratory factor analysis were conducted to
examine the underlying structure of the constructs.

Subsequently, all hypothesised relationships proposed in Chapter 3 were tested. The
model included 13 latent constructs: Streamer’s Attributes, Sense of Presence,
Streamer–Brand–Follower Congruence, Dynamic Brand Experience, Follower
Engagement, Impulse Bu ying, Age, Education, Frequency of Watching, Gender,
Income, and Previous Experience, comprising a total of 67 measurement items. A total
of 18 hypotheses were evaluated, and the results are summarised in Table 15.

236

The following chapter provide an in-depth discussion of these findings, highlighting the
theoretical and practical implications derived from the statistical analysis.
