5 Chapter 6: Discussion and Conclusion
Live streaming commerce has become one of the most disruptive developments in
digital marketing, merging real -time interaction, entertainment, and commerce into a
single consumer experience. Unlike traditional online retail or pre-recorded influencer
promotions, live streaming is characterised by immediacy, interactivity, and social
immersion, creating unique psychological and behavioural dynamics for consumers.
While its commercial success is evident in both China and, increasingly, Western
markets, academ ic understanding of the mechanisms underpinning consumer
responses in these environments is still developing. Therefore, this study provides
timely and novel insights into how streamers’ attributes, congruence, and immersive
experiences influence consumer engagement and impulse buying within live
streaming commerce.

The purpose of this chapter is to move beyond statistical reporting to critically interpret
the results presented in the  data analysis. By situating the results within the wider
scope of literature on digital marketing and consumer psychology, the chapter
highlights how the research contributes to theoretical advancement and practical
application. Specifically, the findings demonstrate that dynamic brand experiences
play a important role in shaping follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour
outcomes, and that presence and congruence act as crucial mediators . These
contributions are important because they extend well -established frameworks—such
as Source Credibility Theory, Congruity Theory, and Flow Theory —into a new, real -
time and cross-cultural context that has so far received limited scholarly attention.

241
A key novelty of this study lies in its multi-theoretical approach. Previous studies have
tended to focus narrowly on individual variables (such as trustworthiness or presence)
or on single -market contexts. In contrast, this research develops and empirical ly
validates an integrated model that integrates multiple theoretical perspectives to
explain consumer engagement and impulse buying in live streaming commerce, while
also comparing patterns between the UK and China. This cross-cultural dimension not
only enhances the generalisability of findings but also illustrates how cultural contexts
may amplify or weaken the impact of psychological mechanisms, an area of enquiry
that has been underexplored in prior research. By foregrounding these contributions,
this chapter underscores how the study moves the literature forward rather than simply
replicating earlier work.

The discussion proceeds in three parts. First, the results are synthesised with existing
theory to highlight how this study supports, contradicts, or extends prior research in
live streaming marketing and consumer behaviour. Second, the chapter draws out the
theoretical and practical implications, emphasising how the results can inform both
academic debates and managerial practice in live streaming commerce. Finally, the
study’s limitations are acknowledged and future research directions proposed, offering
a pathway for subsequent studies to build on these insights. In doing so, this chapter
not only consolidates the contributions of the present research but also points toward
new opportunities for advancing knowledge in this rapidly evolving domain.

5.1  Discussion on Results
This section provides a detailed discussion of the study’s findings, interpreting the
results in light of the conceptual model and prior research. Attention is given to how

242
the empirical evidence supports, refines, or challenges existing theoretical
perspectives on live streaming commerce.

5.1.1 Streamer and Dynamic Brand Experience

Finding 1: Streamers’ attributes positively impact dynamic brand experiences in both
the United Kingdom and China, with the effect being more pronounced among
Chinese followers. Also, this finding answered Objective 1.

This finding highlights the central role of Source Credibility Theory in live streaming
commerce, confirming that followers’ perceptions of a streamer’s credibility shape their
experiential engagement with brands. Importantly, the result is consistent with prior
work (Masuda, Han & Lee, 2022; Lyu, Liang & Vellore Nagarajan, 2024; Xue et al.,
2024), which identified credibility as a reliable predictor of consumer attention and
positive evaluations. However, the present study extends these insights by
demonstrating that attractiveness does not simply function as a superficial trait; rather,
it enhances the immersive quality of th e brand experience by reinforcing a sense of
relational closeness between followers and streamers. This suggests that
attractiveness in live streaming contexts is not just about visual appeal but about
creating affective resonance that strengthens consumer–brand bonds.

Trustworthiness also emerges as a critical dimension, echoing earlier findings (Zhang
et al.2024; Lyu, Liang & Vellore Nagarajan, 2024)  but adding differences
 by showing how trust is socially reinforced in live streaming environments. The results
suggest that followers often look to the broader community within a live stream for

243
cues of shared belief in the streamer’s honesty and reliability. This phenomenon
underscores the collective nature of live streaming commerce: when credibility is
perceived as a shared group consensus, it amplifies the dynamic brand experience
beyond indiv idual evaluation. Such insights align with Congruity Theory, which
suggests that alignment between the follower’s expectations and the streamer’s
perceived values enhances positive responses.

Expertise, the third dimension, provides further evidence of the Stimulus -Organism-
Response (SOR) framework in action  (Yu et al., 2021; Wang et al., 2022; Wei & Xi,
2023). Expertise functions as a cognitive stimulus that reassures followers of product
quality and reduces purchase uncertainty, which in turn enriches the brand
experience. This supports findings by  Xue et al., (2024) and Zhang et al., (2024) but
advances them by situating expertise within the real -time, high-pressure dynamics of
live streaming. In such contexts, followers are not merely evaluating knowledge; they
are interpreting expertise as an assurance mechanism in the absence of physical
product interaction.

Cross-cultural comparison adds an important layer to these findings. The stronger
effect observed in China compared to the UK suggests that collectivist cultural
orientations may heighten the credibility cues. In markets like China, where consumers
are highly accustomed to live streaming commerce, attributes such as trustworthiness
and expertise may carry more weight in shaping immersive experiences. By contrast,
UK participants, representing a relatively newer live commerce market, may be less
influenced by streamer attributes and more re liant on other mechanisms such as
platform credibility or brand reputation. This comparison illustrates how cultural

244
contexts moderate the weight assigned to streamer attributes, advancing our
understanding of live streaming as a global yet culturally embedded phenomenon.

Taken together, these findings extend existing literature by demonstrating that
streamer attributes function not only as surface -level signals but as psychological
mechanisms that actively shape experiential engagement. They provide theoretical
support for Source Credibility Theory and SOR within live streaming commerce while
also offering practical implications for marketers: selecting streamers with high
credibility attributes can substantially enrich brand experiences, but cultural context
must be considered when designing campaigns for international markets.

5.1.2 Streamer’s Attributes Engagement and Impulse buying Behaviour

Finding 2: Streamers’ attributes positively impact follower engagement and impulse
buying in both the United Kingdom and China, although the strength and mechanisms
of influence differ across contexts. Also, this finding answered Objective 1.

This finding confirms the central role of Source Credibility Theory, which posits that
credibility, expertise, and trustworthiness drive follower engagement and impulse
buying behaviour (Hovland & Weiss, 1951). In live streaming commerce, followers who
perceive streamers as credible experts are more likely to engage with the content and
act on product recommendations. This aligns with earlier work  (Wei & Xi, 2023;
Dokumacı, 2024), but extends it by showing how these effects real -time, interactive
environments where credibility functions not only as a rational evaluation but also as
an emotional driver of impulse buying.

245
The results also extend Source Credibility Theory by showing that trustworthiness,
expertise, and attractiveness do not operate in isolation but interact to shape follower
engagement and impulse buying  behaviour in real time. Trustworthiness provides
reassurance, expertise reduces perceived uncertainty, and attractiveness enhances
the emotional appeal of the message. Together, these attributes create what Congruity
Theory describes as an alignment between the streamer, the brand, and the follower,
which strengthens message acceptance and brand attachment(Shen et al., 2022; Jing
& Loang, 2024) . In fast -paced live streaming, where consumers make purchase
decisions under time pressure, such attributes function as cognitive and emotional
shortcuts, enabling followers to engage quickly and act impulsively without prolonged
deliberation.

Cultural differences deepen this interpretation. In China, followers’ higher trust in
streamers reflects the collectivist cultural norm of relying on authority and social proof
(Luo et al., 2021; Chen, Zhao & Wang, 2022; Chu, 2024). Here, streamers are not just
entertainers but also perceived as authoritative figures, which amplifies impulse
buying. Conversely, in the UK, where individualism and independent evaluation are
stronger (Yang, 2022; Zhao & Chiu, 2023), followers are more likely to seek additional
verification before purchasing. In this context, streamers’ attractiveness appears to
play a relatively stronger role in capturing attention and driving engagement (Zhao &
Chiu, 2023; Wang & Wang, 2024) , while credibility cues are integrated with other
information sources.

This comparative perspective highlights both universal and context -specific
mechanisms. Universally, credibility and expertise matter across cultures;

246
contextually, the reliance on authority (China) versus personal judgment (UK) shifts
the relative influence of different attributes. This not only enriches the application of
Source Credibility Theory in live streaming commerce but also integrates it with cross-
cultural psychology, showing how cultural orientations moderate the pathways from
credibility to behaviour.

From a practical perspective, the findings suggest that brands should carefully tailor
their streamer partnerships. In China, credibility and authority should be emphasised,
as followers are more likely to respond to streamers perceived as trusted advisors. In
the UK, a combination of attractiveness, authenticity, and transparent information may
be more effective, given consumers’ preference for independent evaluation.

5.1.3 Sense of Presence Enhancing the Dynamic Brand Experience

Finding 3: Sense of presence significantly mediates the relationship between
streamers’ attributes and dynamic brand experience among Chinese participants but
is negligible in the UK. This highlights cross-cultural differences in how immersion and
interaction shape c onsumer responses in live streaming environments and directly
addresses Research Objective 2.

The findings reveal that sense of presence —comprising both social presence and
telepresence—mediates the relationship between streamers’ attributes and dynamic
brand experience significantly in China but not in the UK. This cultural difference
highlights the varying importance of immersive feeling mechanisms across consumer
groups.

247
In China, social presence —the perception of personal connection with the streamer
and other viewers (Ming et al., 2021; Zahid et al., 2024)  —is a key driver of brand
experience. This aligns with prior research suggesting that collectivist cultural
orientations place greater value on interpersonal bonds and community within digital
settings (Li et al., 2022; Xu, Kapitan & Phillips, 2023; Li et al., 2024) . When followers
perceive strong social interaction, they become more engaged with the brand
narrative, reinforcing the impact of streamer credibility. By contrast, UK participants,
shaped by more individualistic orientations, appear less influenced by the  “social
warmth” of the live streaming session, suggesting that engagement in these markets
may rely more on informational content or streamer expertise than on relational cues.

Similarly, telepresence —the sense of being virtually immersed in the streaming
environment—exerts a stronger influence in China, where audiences are accustomed
to highly interactive and gamified platform features (Barta, Gurrea & Flavián, 2023;
Zahid et al., 2024) . This finding underscores the importance of technological
affordances: Chinese platforms like Taobao and Douyin provide more immersive
design elements than Western counterparts, which may explain why telepresence did
not significantly enhance brand experiences among UK participants.

Theoretically, these findings contribute by demonstrating that sense of presence
operates as a culture -contingent psychological mechanism in live  streaming
commerce. This extends Flow Theory by showing that immersion (telepresence) and
relational warmth (social presence) are not universally influential but are instead
conditioned by cultural and platform factors. Practically, the results imply that
marketers must adapt presence -based strategies to cultural contexts: in China,

248
enhancing interaction features and immersive design may be critical, while in the UK,
strategies may need to focus on credibility signals and informative content rather than
attempting to replicate the same sense of presence.

5.1.4 Sense of Presence Enhancing Engagement and Impulse Buying Behaviour

Finding 4: The sense of presence served as a mediating variable in the research,
indirectly and positively affecting followers’ engagement and impulse buying through
dynamic brand experience from China and indirectly but not affecting the United
Kingdom users, this is due to the differences between the live streaming platforms in
China and the UK, which lead to different functions. UK live streaming platforms do
not have more interactive functions, which leads to indirect and negative impacts on
follower engagement an d impulse buying . Also, this  finding answered the research
Objective 2.

This finding reveals the differences between countries in how the sense of presence
functions as a mediating variable between streamers' attributes and dynamic brand
experience, ultimately influencing follower engagement and impulse buying in the UK
and China. While the sense of presence positively m ediates this relationship among
Chinese participants, it shows that it cannot have a positive mediating effect among
UK participants. This suggests that the ability of live streaming to create a strong sense
of immersion and engagement differs significantly between the two markets, likely due
to variations in platform design, user expectations, and interactive functionalities.

249
For UK participants, this research found that the sense of presence cannot impact the
dynamic brand experience, meaning that a weaker or less engaging presence
experience indirectly led to lower follower engagement and impulse buying. A possible
explanation for this is the difference in live streaming platform capabilities between the
UK and China. Even within TikTok, which operates globally, the Chinese version
(Douyin) is more advanced in terms of interactive features, such as real -time
engagement tools, gamification elements, and highly immersive li ve streaming
functionalities. By contrast, the international version of TikTok and other Western live-
streaming platforms offer fewer real -time interactive elements, which may result in a
diminished sense of presence for UK consumers (Yang, 2022; He et al., 2023; koshi,
2023; Wang et al., 2024; Xin, Liu & Jian, 2024). As a result, UK followers may perceive
the live streaming experience as less immersive, leading to a weaker dynamic brand
experience and reduced engagement.

Conversely, in China, the study found that the sense of presence positively mediates
the relationship between streamers' attributes and dynamic brand experience, thereby
increasing follower engagement and impulse buying.  This is consistent with previous
research results (Wang et al., 2022; Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023) . This
indicates that Chinese consumers are more likely to feel a stronger sense of presence
during live streaming sessions, which enhances their overall brand experience. One
potential explanation for this finding is that Chinese live streaming platforms,  such as
Douyin, Taobao Live, and Kuaishou, have significantly more integrated engagement
mechanisms, including real -time interaction, pop -up comments, virtual gifting, live
polling, and seamless e -commerce integration (Guo et al., 2021; Kang et al., 2021;
Chen & Yang, 2023) . These interactive elements likely contribute to heightened

250
immersion, making consumers feel more connected to the streamer and increasing
their trust in product recommendations (Li, Feng & Zhao, 2023; Ji, Chen & Wei, 2025).
Consequently, this enhanced presence experience fosters greater engagement and
increases the likelihood of impulse purchases.

All in all, these insights underscore the critical role of platform interactivity and
technological capabilities in shaping consumer experiences in live streaming
commerce. The contrasting findings between the UK and China emphasi se the need
for culturally adaptive live streaming strategies, with a stronger focus on enhancing
social presence and telepresence in Western markets to replicate the success seen
in China’s highly immersive live streaming commerce ecosystem.

5.1.5 Brand, Streamer and Follower Congruence

Key Finding 5:  Brand, streamer, and follower congruence positively mediate the
relationship between streamers’ attributes and dynamic brand experience,
demonstrating that alignment between brand identity, streamer persona, and follower
preferences enhances engagement an d positively influences follower engagement
and impulse buying behaviour across both the United Kingdom and China. Also, this
finding answered Research Objective 2

Key Finding 6: Streamer, brand, and followers’ congruence positively and indirectly
affect followers’ engagement and impulse buying through dynamic brand experience
in the UK and China. That demonstrates that congruence between brand identity,
streamer persona, and follower preferences indirectly enhances follower engagement

251
and impulse buying in the United Kingdom and China. Also, this finding answered
research Objective 2.

This study extends prior work (Liu, Zhang & Zhang, 2020; Maghraoui & Khrouf, 2024)
by showing that congruence is not a peripheral construct but a central psychological
mechanism in live streaming commerce. When followers perceive consistency
between the brand, the streamer, and themselves, the brand message appears more
authentic, leadi ng to stronger immersion in the brand experience and greater
responsiveness to purchasing cues.

Breaking this down further, brand congruence refers to the degree of alignment
between the brand’s identity and the streamer’s persona. For example, a brand that
partners with a streamer whose expertise or attractiveness reflects its values
strengthens message authenticity and credibility (Wei et al., 2022; Hou & Hou, 2024).
Streamer congruence captures the alignment between the streamer and follower
expectations—when followers identify with the streamer’s lifestyle, values, or
demographics, they are more likely to perceive the brand as relevant and to engage
actively (Shehzala et al., 2024; Gopakumar & Dananjayan, 2024) . Finally, follower
congruence describes the extent to which audiences share similar values or interests,
fostering a sense of community. This collective identity reinforces the impact of both
the streamer and the brand, creating a socially validated and i mmersive brand
experience (Alnoor et al., 2024; Maghraoui & Khrouf, 2024).

Together, these three dimensions of congruence act as mutually reinforcing
mechanisms that convert individual perceptions into collective brand experiences. The

252
consistency of these effects across both cultural contexts suggests that congruence
is a universally relevant construct in live streaming commerce, making it highly
significant for both theoretical development and managerial practice. For theory, it
highlights congruence as a key mediating mechanism connecting credibility -based
streamer attributes to follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour. For
practice, it underscores the importance of selecting streamers who not only fit the
brand but also resonate with the target audience and foster a sense of shared identity
among followers.

5.1.6  Dynamic Brand Experience Engagement and Impulse Buying Behaviour

Finding 7: Dynamic brand experience positively impacts follower engagement and
impulse buying behaviour in live streaming commerce environments, with consistent
effects across both the United Kingdom and China. This addresses Research
Objective 2.

The results of this study highlight the central role of dynamic brand experience in
shaping follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour within live streaming
commerce. Rather than simply acting as a background variable, dynamic brand
experience emerges as a important mechanism through which streamers’ attributes
and interactive environments are translated into follower engagement and impulse
buying behaviour. This finding contributes to the broader literature by confirming that
immersive brand experiences are not only correlated with engagement and purchasing
but also provide a direct psychological pathway that connects live interactions to
impulsive decisions.

253
Dynamic brand experience was conceptualised in this study as a multidimensional
construct, comprising affective, sensory, behavioural, intellectual, and consistent
dimensions (Yang et al., 2017; Legendre, Cartier & Warnick, 2019; Yu et al., 2021; Yin
et al., 2022; Henkel & Toporowski, 2023; Park, Hong & Park, 2023; Shahid Satar et
al., 2023). The findings show that these elements collectively foster deeper cognitive
and emotional engagement, which in turn stimulates spontaneous purchasing
behaviours. For example, affective and sensory cues—such as excitement, enjoyment,
or product demonstrati ons—were found to enhance emotional resonance with the
brand. This supports prior research (Gu, Cheng & Shen, 2023; Wei & Xi, 2023), which
argued that emotional arousal is a key driver of impulsive behaviour, while also
extending it by showing that such effects are consistent across both UK and Chinese
contexts.

Similarly, behavioural and intellectual dimensions of dynamic brand experience were
found to encourage followers to participate actively in live streams, ask questions, and
critically evaluate products in real time. This interactive involvement not only bu ilds
stronger brand connections but also lowers decision-making barriers, making impulse
purchases more likely. These results align with prior studies (Yang et al., 2017; Yin et
al., 2022; Bawack et al., 2023)  that emphasised the role of active consumer
participation in fostering brand loyalty, but extend them by demonstrating that the
immediacy of live streaming amplifies the translation of engagement into direct
purchase behaviours.

Importantly, this study also reveals that dynamic brand experience functions as a
universal mechanism across cultures. Both UK and Chinese followers responded

254
positively to rich brand experiences, although the underlying triggers may differ:
Chinese followers appeared more strongly influenced by collective social presence
and trust in streamers, while UK followers were more driven by sensory appeal and
consistent product presentation. This highlights the robustness of dynamic brand
experience as a cross -cultural driver of follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour, while also suggesting that the weighting of its dimensions may vary
depending on cultural norms and platform affordances.

Overall, these findings demonstrate that dynamic brand experience is not merely an
outcome of live streaming commerce but an essential bridge between streamer
influence and consumer action. By creating immersive, emotionally resonant, and
participatory environments, dynamic brand experiences elevate both engagement and
impulse buying, underscoring their importance for both theory and practice in digital
marketing.

5.1.7 Product Type and Gender
Finding 8:  The moderating role of gender and product type does not impact the
relationships between dynamic brand experience, followers’ engagement, and
impulse buying in the UK and China.  This finding answers Research Objective 3.

This suggests that regardless of gender or the type of product being promoted, the
effect of dynamic brand experience on follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour remains consistent across both cultural contexts. In other words,
consumers’ engagement with a brand and their likelihood of making impulse

255
purchases are driven by their perception of the dynamic brand experience itself rather
than demographic factors or product categories.

This result is notable because it challenges assumptions from traditional consumer
behaviour research, where demographic factors such as gender and product type are
often found to influence purchasing patterns and engagement (e.g., Liu & Yu, 2022).
In the context of live streaming commerce, however, the findings suggest that dynamic
brand experience exerts a universally strong influence that overrides demographic
distinctions. Regardless of whether the product is fashion, food, or technology —and
irrespective of whether the follower is male or female —the immersive quality of the
brand experience itself drives engagement and spontaneous purchasing.

One possible explanation for this finding is that dynamic brand experience is a
universally engaging factor that transcends individual differences such as gender or
product type. Prior research has shown that immersive and interactive brand
experiences enh ance consumer engagement by triggering emotional and cognitive
responses, making the consumption experience more memorable and stimulating
(Brodie et al., 2011; Schmitt, 1999). This effect appears to hold across diverse
followers, meaning that consumers, irrespective of their gender identity or the product
being promoted, respond similarly when exposed to a strong and compelling brand
experience.

Additionally, this finding may reflect the evolution of follower engagement and impulse
buying behaviour in live streaming commerce, where traditional segmentation factors
such as gender and product type are becoming less influential in shaping consumer

256
engagement(Liu & Yu, 2022, Anon, 2023). The algorithm of digital platforms allows for
personalised content, where content is tailored to the follower’s interests  and watch
history rather than broad demographic categories. This shift suggests that merchants
or live streamers cannot pre-screen potential customer groups because live streaming
content is algorithmically pushed by the  platform based on consumers' browsing
history, shopping habits, and viewing history. Therefore, live streamers should focus
more on content rather than traditional demographic classifications.

Moreover, the cross-cultural consistency of this result highlights the globalised nature
of live streaming commerce, where consumer engagement patterns are increasingly
shaped by digital interaction dynamics rather than sociocultural norms. While gender
and product type have historically influenced purchasing behaviour in traditional retail
and e -commerce settings, live streaming commerce fosters real -time engagement,
social interaction, and immersive brand storytelling, which may override these
demographic distinctions.

Theoretically, these advances in understanding show that the psychological
mechanisms underpinning live streaming commerce may differ from those in offline or
static digital contexts. Whereas segmentation by gender and product category has
long been a cornerstone of marketing strategy, these results point to the primacy of
experiential drivers such as emotional immersion, interactivity, and authenticity
(Brodie et al., 2011; Schmitt, 1999). This finding extends the literature by
demonstrating that in real-time, interactive environments, consumers converge in their
behavioural responses, suggesting a shift toward more universalised patterns of digital
engagement.

257
From a practical perspective, this means that marketers and streamers may not need
to design fundamentally different live-streaming strategies for men versus women, or
for specific product categories. Instead, investment should be directed toward
enhancing the quality of the overall brand experience —through interactive tools,
emotional storytelling, and engaging content design —which appears to have a
stronger and more consistent effect on follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour than traditional segmentation. In an increasingly globalised and digitalised
marketplace, these findings reinforce the idea that experience trumps demographics
in shaping engagement and purchase decisions.

5.2 Managerial Implications
The above summarises some of the results from the academic side through data
analysis, however, there are also some findings from the practical side  to answer
Research Objective 4 . Based on the findings of this study, several practical
recommendations could be made for digital marketers, live streaming platforms, and
brands to enhance consumer engagement, impulse purchase s, and optimize the
effectiveness of live streaming commerce.

First, brands should carefully align streamer attributes with their brand identity and
target followers. The findings indicate that streamers’ expertise, trustworthiness, and
attractiveness strongly influence consumer engagement and impulse buying across
both the UK and China. Therefore, brands should select streamers whose style,
communication approach, an d follower demographics align with their brand image.
Instead of randomly choosing streamers based on popularity, brands should conduct

258
in-depth audience analysis to ensure that the streamer resonates with the intended
consumer base, leading to more authentic and effective brand endorsements.

Second, live-streaming platforms and digital marketers should focus on enhancing the
sense of presence to increase follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour.
The study highlights the significant role of social presence and telepresence,
particularly in China, where immersive and interactive live streaming experiences
positively influence follower behaviour.  However, the UK market still has great
potential for development in this area . To replicate this success in UK markets,
platform developers should integrate advanced interactive features such as real -time
chat, virtual gifting, personali sed recommendations, and augmented reality elements
to create a more engaging and immersive shopping environment.

Third, streamers should actively engage with their followers to enhance the dynamic
brand experience. The study suggests that interactive elements play a crucial role in
driving engagement and impulse buying, particularly in China, where platforms are
more interactive. Streamers should focus on direct communication with viewers,
responding to questions in real -time, initiating discussions, and incorp orating
gamification techniques such as exclusive deals, countdown sales, and follower -
driven content. This strategy not only strengthens the relationship between the
streamer and the followers but also enhances consumer trust and purchase intent.

Fourth, marketers should prioritise the quality of the dynamic brand experience over
traditional demographic segmentation. The study found that gender and product type
do not significantly moderate consumer engagement and impulse buying in live

259
streaming commerce, indicating that followers are more influenced by their perception
of the streamer and brand experience itself rather than their demographic
characteristics. Therefore, instead of relying on gender -based marketing strategies,
brands should invest in creating highly engaging, sensory -driven, and interactive live
streaming experiences that appeal to a broader audience in the UK and China.
Fifth, future digital marketing strategies should incorporate cross -cultural
considerations to tailor live streaming experiences for different markets. The findings
suggest that Chinese consumers respond more positively to social presence and
telepresence, whereas UK consumers may require improvements in interactivity to
enhance their engagement levels. Brands and live streaming platforms should
consider these cultural differences when developing locali sed live streaming
strategies, ensuring that their approach is adapted to regional consumer behaviours
and technological expectations.

By implementing these practical recommendations, brands, streamers, and platform
developers could optimise their live-streaming commerce strategies, increase follower
engagement, and drive higher impulse purchasing behaviours across diverse markets.

5.3  Contribution
This research makes a number of  theoretical and practical contributions to the
literature on live streaming commerce and the literature on streamers, dynamic brand
experiences and follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour. Overall, this
research advances a unified framework that links streamers’ attributes, sense of
presence, congruence, and dynamic brand experience to explain consumer
engagement and impulse buying in live streaming commerce. By integrating these

260
constructs, the study demonstrates how psychological mechanisms (e.g., emotional
involvement through presence and cognitive alignment through congruence) connect
streamer characteristics to follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour. This
integrative perspective extends existing digital marketing theories by showing that
consumer responses in live streaming are not shaped by isolated factors, but by the
combined influence of interactivity, alignment, and immersion within dynamic bran d
experiences. Those are explained in detail in the following paragraphs.

5.3.1  Theoretical Contribution
5.3.1.1 Extends Streamers’ Attributes Literature
Addressing Research Gap 3, this research extends the literature on streamers’
attributes by examining their relationship with sense of presence and congruence  in
the unique context of live streaming commerce. Prior studies have focused on
attributes such as expertise, trustworthiness, and attractiveness in relation to
consumer trust (Yan et al., 2022; Zhou & Lou, 2023; Hou & Hou, 2024; Alnoor et al.,
2024; Li, Chen & Zhu, 2024) and flow experiences (Zheng et al., 2023). However, the
ways in which these attributes interact with the sense of presence and congruence to
shape sense of dynamic brand experiences have been largely overlooked. This study
addresses this gap by proposing and empirically testing a comprehensive model in
which presence (social presence and telepresence) and congruence (brand, streamer,
and follower alignment) serve as key mediators linking streamer attributes to dynamic
brand experiences.

The findings demonstrate that. Social and telepresence stimulate emotional
involvement and cognitive absorption, while congruence reinforces perceived

261
authenticity and value alignment, together amplifying the overall brand experience. In
doing so, this research highlights the distinctiveness of live streaming commerce as a
digital environment—marked by interactivity (Ming et al., 2021; Shi, Li & Hu, 2023; Li,
Xuan & Chen, 2024) and immersion (Liao et al., 2022; Ma, Zou & Lv, 2022; Shi, Li &
Hu, 2023; Li, Xuan & Chen, 2024) —that differentiates it from traditional social media
or static e-commerce settings.

In contrast to earlier studies that suggested streamer attributes such as expertise or
interactivity may have little direct impact on purchase intentions  (Zhao et al., 2024) ,
this research shows that their influence operates indirectly through presence and
congruence. By validating this model across both the UK and China, the study also
enhances the cross-cultural generalisability of these findings. Thus, it advances theory
by clarifying how streamer attributes contribute to consumer experiences—not only by
shaping trust or attractiveness directly, but by activating mediating psychological
mechanisms that create immersive, credible, and engaging brand encounters.

5.3.1.2 Contribution to SOR Theory
Reply to Research Gap 1, grounded in flow theory and the stimulus -organism-
response (SOR) model of sense of presence, which encompasses both social
presence and telepresence, recent literature emphasises the influence of these
dimensions on follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour (Ming et al., 2021;
Ma, Zou & Lv, 2022; Zhang, Cheng & Huang, 2023; Li, Xuan & Chen, 2024) . Despite
this, limited attention has been given to their role in shaping Dynamic Brand
Experience within the context of live streaming commerce. Addressing this research
gap, this study elaborates on the mediating role of a sense of presence between

262
streamers' attributes and dynamic brand experience, thereby contributing
substantively to the fields of digital marketing and live streaming commerce.

Also, this research provides robust empirical evidence highlighting the impact of social
presence and telepresence as key mechanisms that enhance or modify dynamic
brand experiences and follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour during
live-streaming interactions. Notably, while prior research has suggested that
telepresence may yield negative responses in live -streaming commerce (Barta,
Gurrea & Flavián, 2023), this research demonstrates a contrasting outcome, revealing
a positive influence of telepresence and social presence among Chinese participants
but not significant impact within the United Kingdom context. This difference from
earlier findings underscores the importance of cultural context in shaping consumer
experiences. Furthermore , by extending SOR model and Flow theory with the
integrated dimensions of telepresence and social presence, this study offers a more
comprehensive understanding of the relationship between dynamic brand experiences
and follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour. This integrative approach
enriches existing theoretical frameworks and advances knowledge on how immersive
and interactive elements in live streaming could shape consumer -brand interactions
(Ming et al., 2021; Huo et al., 2023; Shi, Li & Hu, 2023).

5.3.1.3 Extension of Congruence Theory
Addressing the identified Research Gap 1, this research extends beyond prior
investigations that have predominantly explored isolated relationships —such as
brand-consumer purchase congruence (Ji, Liu & Chen, 2023; Wang, 202 4; Zhang &
Zhang, 2024) or live streamer-consumer engagement congruence (Wei et al., 2022)

263
—by examining the integrated influence of streamer, follower, and brand congruence
on dynamic brand experiences. This multidimensional approach empirically validates
that congruence among these elements serves as a mediating factor linking
streamers’ attributes to dynamic brand experiences in both the United Kingdom and
China. This finding significantly broadens the theoretical understanding of how the
congruence across these dimensions mediates the streamers’ attributes and dynamic
brand experiences within the live streaming commerce context, thereby creating a new
literature that contributes to the fields of live streaming commerce and digital
marketing.

Besides, unlike previous research, which has often been restricted to specific
industries, platforms, or regions (Wei et al., 2022; Ji, Liu & Chen, 2023; Wu et al.,
2023; Wang, 2024), this research adopts a broader scope by including a diverse range
of streamers from various platforms and geographical locations. This inclusive
approach increases the reliability, generali sability, and applicability of the findings
across different countries. The research underscores how congruence at multiple
levels—streamer-brand alignment, streamer -follower engagement, and follower -
brand perception —cohesively mediates the relationship bet ween the streamer’s
attributes and dynamic brand experiences, offering a comprehensive view that had
been missing in previous literature.

Moreover, building on and extending existing theoretical frameworks, such as
research on Wang,(2024) this study, fills a critical gap by incorporating congruence
theory to better explain brand -live streamer-follower interactions. The findings thus
offer a more comprehensive perspective on how congruence drives meaningful

264
consumer interactions, enhancing both theoretical models in live streaming
commerce.

5.3.1.4 Advance Dynamic Brand Experience Literature
Addressing research gap 2 and 5 , this research extends the existing literature on
dynamic brand experience by empirically demonstrating its mediating role in the
relationship between sense of presence, congruence, and follower engagement and
impulse buying  behaviour within live streaming commerce. While previous studies
have explored the concept of dynamic brand experience, they have primarily focused
on single social media platforms such as Weibo, Instagram, and Twitter, often limiting
their scope to static brand consumer interactions (Yin et al., 2022; Park, Hong & Park,
2023). This study bridges this gap by situating dynamic brand experiences within the
live streaming commerce context, a highly interactive and rapidly evolving digital
marketing environment.

Moreover, while earlier research has frequently examined the relationship between
brand experiences and brand loyalty or customer retention (Kumar & Kaushik, 2020;
Yin et al., 2022; Park, Hong & Park, 2023; Valmohammadi et al., 2024; Wang, 2024),
few have integrated dynamic brand experiences into impulse buying and follower
engagement. Importantly, this research is among the first to investigate how dynamic
brand experience mediates the effects of sense of presence (social and telepresence)
and brand, streamer, and follower congruence on follower engagement and impulse
buying in a live streaming scenario. This represents a critical advancement in
understanding how dynamic brand experiences function as a mechanism that

265
enhances follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour in digital marketing and
live streaming environments.

The findings reveal that dynamic brand experience positively influences both follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviours across the United Kingdom and China,
reinforcing its universal importance in live streaming commerce. By empirically
validating the mediating role of dynamic brand experience, this research provides a
comprehensive understanding of how interactive and immersive elements within live
streaming amplify consumer responses, offering theoretical insights into live streaming
commerce.

In addition to expanding the theoretical understanding of dynamic brand experience,
this research underscores its central role in driving positive consumer outcomes,
challenging the traditional focus on static brand-consumer relationships. By integrating
dynamic brand experience into the framework of live streaming commerce, this
research provides substantial evidence for its relevance in enhancing follower
engagement and purchase behaviours, offering valuable implications for future studies
and practical strategies in the field of digital marketing and live streaming commerce.

5.3.2  Practical Contribution
The contributions of this study provide valuable practical insights for the digital
marketing industry, particularly within the emerging field of live streaming commerce.
A central challenge for practitioners is how to effectively attract viewers, foster
meaningful interaction, and build trust in order to convert  engagement into actual

266
purchases. The findings of this research address these concerns by linking them to
the underlying theoretical framework of the study.

Grounded in the Stimulus –Organism–Response (SOR) framework, the results show
that when streamers’ attributes (stimulus) trigger psychological mechanisms such as
a sense of presence and brand –streamer–follower congruence (organism), the
consumer’s dynamic b rand experience (response) is significantly enhanced. This
stronger brand experience, in turn, drives higher levels of follower engagement and
impulse buying. From a practical perspective, this means that companies should not
view live streaming merely as a sales channel, but as an experiential environment in
which psychological immersion and authenticity are key levers for influencing follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviour.

The findings also extend signalling theory, suggesting that congruence between brand
identity, streamer persona, and follower values functions as a credibility signal. For
practitioners, this highlights the importance of carefully selecting streamers whose
expertise, style, and values authentically represent the brand. Random or mismatched
partnerships risk decreasing trust, whereas congruent partnerships amplify credibility,
deepen engagement, and increase the likelihood of spontaneous purchases.

Moreover, this study reinforces insights from flow theory by showing that interactive
and immersive live streaming environments foster emotional involvement and
cognitive absorption. For practitioners, this underlines the need to design live sessions
that go beyond one-way product promotion. Instead, streamers should encourage two-

267
way interaction (e.g., real -time Q&A, virtual gifting, polls, or gamified features) to
heighten immersion and trigger impulse buying.

The UK–China comparison further highlights the importance of cultural sensitivity in
managerial strategy. In China, where collectivist values and high digital familiarity
prevail, consumers place greater trust in streamers’ expertise and respond strongly to
interactive features. Platforms such as Douyin or Taobao Live are designed to
maximise presence and interactivity, which explains why Chinese followers report
higher levels of engagement and impulse buying. In contrast, in the UK, with its more
individualistic culture, followers are comparatively less influenced by social presence
and tend to place greater emphasis on independent evaluation and source credibility.
This suggests that Western marketers should focus on transparency, clear product
information, and authenticity to build trust, while simultaneously investing in platform
features that enhance immersion to narrow the experiential gap with Chinese
platforms. More details are discussed below

5.3.2.1 Impacts Related to Social Presence
The findings of this research highlight the critical role of social presence —the feeling
of being personally connected and socially engaged during live streaming
interactions—in shaping follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour within
live streaming commerce. While social presence was found to positively influence
consumer engagement and impulse buying in both the UK and China, its effect was
stronger among Chinese participants. This reflects cultural differences: in collectivist
contexts such as China, social connectedness and community -oriented experiences

268
are more highly valued, whereas UK participants, situated in a more individualistic
context, respond positively but with less intensity.

From a practical perspective, these results suggest that live streamers and digital
marketing companies should prioritise strategies that foster a heightened sense of
social presence during live sessions, thereby reinforcing engagement and trust.
Grounded in the SOR framew ork, social presence functions as a psychological
“organism” that transforms the streamer’s attributes (stimuli) into a richer consumer
response by encouraging emotional involvement and interpersonal closeness.

Practical strategies for enhancing social presence include:
• Interactive communication: Streamers should go beyond product descriptions
by actively responding to follower questions, initiating conversational prompts,
and recognising individual audience members during the session. These micro-
interactions make followers feel noticed and valued, thereby deepening social
bonds.
• Personalised acknowledgement: Addressing viewers by name, responding to
comments in real time, and using inclusive language fosters a participatory
environment that enhances the consumer’s perception of being part of a
community.
• Balancing product focus with engagement: Rather than focusing exclusively on
sales, streamers should balance informative content with interactive dialogue,
which builds trust and strengthens brand associations.

269
The results further indicate that fostering social presence not only enhances the
dynamic brand experience but also improves followers’ perceptions of product
authenticity and brand credibility, ultimately increasing engagement and impulse
purchase behaviour. This effect was particularly visible among Chinese participants,
where interactive features such as live chat, gifting, and real -time reactions are
integrated deeply into platform design. By contrast, UK platforms provide fewer
interactive functions, suggesting that Western marketers may need to innovate around
interactivity (e.g., polls, Q&A features, or gamified engagement) to replicate the higher
levels of presence achieved in China.

So, building a strong social presence is essential for driving follower engagement and
impulse buying  behaviour in live streaming commerce. By embedding interactivity,
personalised communication, and community -building strategies into live streaming
sessions, streamers and marketers can create memorable experiences that enhance
engagement and stimulate impulse buyi ng across diverse cultural contexts.
Furthermore, these contributions, grounded in the SOR framework, extend the scope
of social presence research by showing how it not only enhances engagement in
general digital environments but also specifically drives impulse buying in the unique
context of live streaming commerce (Ming et al., 2021; Zahid et al., 2024).

5.3.2.2 Impacts Related to Telepresence
The findings of this research reveal that telepresence —the psychological state of
feeling virtually present in a mediated environment—plays a important role in shaping
followers’ perceptions of dynamic brand experiences. A heightened sense of
telepresence was shown to increase both engagement and impulse buying behaviour,

270
underscoring its strategic importance for live streamers and digital marketing
companies. Grounded in Flow Theory and the SOR framework, telepresence
enhances immersion (the “organism”), which transforms external stimuli (streamer
communication, platform d esign) into deeper consumer responses (active
engagement and spontaneous purchasing).

• From a practical perspective, live streamers and digital marketers should focus
on strategies that maximise immersion during broadcasts, enabling followers to
feel as though they are “inside” the streaming environment. Effective
techniques include:
• Vivid verbal communication: Streamers can use descriptive language,
metaphors, and storytelling to stimulate the imagination, helping followers
visualise products and experiences in a lifelike manner.
• Dynamic non-verbal cues: Gestures, facial expressions, and vocal modulation
enhance realism and help simulate a sense of co-presence with the streamer.
• Multi-sensory framing: Describing how a product feels, looks, or sounds creates
a layered sensory experience that strengthens the illusion of “being there.”

The results also highlight a cultural difference. Chinese participants reported a
stronger telepresence effect, reflecting both the advanced immersive features of
Chinese live streaming platforms (e.g., real -time gifting, interactive overlays,
gamification) and cultural preferences for highly interactive, community-oriented online
environments. By contrast, UK participants reported weaker effects, likely due to fewer
platform-based immersive functions and more cautious consumer engagement styles.
For Wester n marketers, this suggests a need to experiment with creative design

271
solutions—such as live polls, interactive product trials, or augmented reality
integrations—to replicate the intensity of telepresence observed in China.

Overall, this study extends Flow Theory by demonstrating that telepresence in live
streaming is not only about immersion but also functions as a mechanism linking
cognitive absorption with consumer action (Ki et al., 2024).” Also, this study
demonstrates that telepresence is not just a technical artefact but a psychological
mechanism that drives follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour in live
streaming commerce. By deliberately cultivating immersive experiences through
language, performance, and platform design, marketers and streamers can enrich the
dynamic brand experience, foster stronger emotional and cognitive bonds with the
brand, and ultimately stimulate higher levels of engagement and impulse purchasing.

5.3.2.3 Impacts Related to Congruence
By applying Congruence Theory to the live streaming context, this study extends its
applicability beyond traditional brand–consumer alignment to a triadic model involving
streamer, brand, and followers. The findings of this research demonstrate that
congruence—the alignment between the streamer’s persona, the brand’s identity, and
the followers’ values —plays a critical role in enhancing dynamic brand experiences
across both China and the United Kingdom. T his alignment significantly strengthens
follower engagement and impulse buying, underlining the importance of selecting
streamers and brands whose identities naturally resonate with the target audience.
Grounded in Congruence Theory and the SOR framework, congruence functions as a
psychological mechanism: it reduces cognitive dissonance, fosters perceptions of

272
authenticity, and enhances emotional connection, thereby making the overall brand
experience more compelling.

From a practical perspective, this means that matching matters. Streamers and digital
marketing companies should carefully analyse the streamer’s persona and style (e.g.,
luxury-oriented, lifestyle-focused, technology-driven) alongside the demographics and
psychographics of their followers to ensure the chosen brand or product aligns
seamlessly with both. For example, a mismatch —such as a luxury fashion streamer
promoting low-cost budget travel—can reduce authenticity and weaken engagement.
By contrast, alignment between a tech streamer and a consumer electronics brand, or
a lifestyle influencer and wellness products, creates a sense of relevance that
amplifies trust and stimulates consumer action.

The findings also highlight subtle cultural differences. In China’s collectivist digital
environment, congruence tends to be reinforced by community dynamics: when
followers perceive alignment between streamer, brand, and group values, the effect
on engagement and impulse buying is amplified. In the UK, however, with its more
individualistic consumer culture, congruence is often judged at the personal level —
followers are more sensitive to authenticity and may disengage quickly if they perceive
a promotional  partnership as forced or commercially motivated. These distinctions
suggest that while congruence is universally important, its strength may vary by
cultural context and consumer expectations.

Therefore, to optimise live streaming campaigns, brands and streamers should curate
partnerships that feel natural, authentic, and relevant to both the audience and the

273
product category. By prioritising brand–streamer–follower alignment, digital marketers
can foster cohesive, immersive brand experiences that enhance trust, deepen
engagement, and drive impulse buying purchases, regardless of cultural context.

5.4 Limitations and Future Research
This research explores the impact of different dimensions of sense of presence and
congruence, as well as their relationship with streamers’ attributes and dynamic brand
experience in live streaming commerce. Additionally, it examines how dynamic brand
experience influences followers' engagement and impulse buying behaviour. While
this research offers valuable insights into the interplay between streamers, consumers,
and brands in the context of online live streaming commerce, it is not without
limitations.

As with any research, certain constraints must be acknowledged, which also provide
opportunities for further exploration. These limitations do not undermine the
significance of the findings but rather highlight areas where future research could build
on th is research to deepen understanding in the growing field of live streaming
commerce. The following section outlines these limitations and suggests avenues for
future inquiry to address them.

5.4.1 Cultural Variability
5.4.1.1 Limitation
This research examines the impact of the sense of presence, incorporating
dimensions such as social presence and telepresence, as outlined in the models
developed by Sun et al.(2019) and further extended by Shi, Li & Hu, (2023) and Tian

274
& Frank, (2024). Prior research has established that the sense of presence is a critical
factor influencing consumer behaviour in live streaming commerce. Some studies
demonstrate that a heightened sense of presence enhances user trust (Ma, Zou & Lv,
2022), satisfaction and stickiness Barta, Gurrea & Flavián, 2023) , and consumer
engagement (Xue et al., 2020) . For example, Ma, Zou & Lv (2022)  and Zhang et al.
(2022) highlighting that social presence, which fosters a sense of connection with the
streamer, plays a important role in building trust. When consumers feel actively
engaged with the streamer, such as through personalised interactions and responsive
communication, they perceive the streamer as trustworthy, leading to a positive brand
experience and higher levels of engagement.

However, this research does not explicitly examine some of the finer aspects identified
in recent studies. For example, variables such as colour schemes, visual impact, and
the streamer’s interactive style have been shown to significantly contribute to
enhancing social presence and telepresence. These sensory and stylistic cues could
create a more immersive environment for viewers, strengthening their connection to
the brand and their overall engagement, but they were not included in this study’s
measurement.

Additionally, while this research controlled for demographic variables such as age,
gender, level of education, and employment status, it did not account for how visual
aesthetics, streamer presentation styles, and cultural preferences might mediate or
enhance the sense of presence. Importantly, the research’s scope does not address
the potential cultural difference between the UK and Chinese participants, particularly
in their preferences for visual elements, communication styles, and interaction

275
patterns. Cultural differences in colour symbolism, engagement strategies, and
follower expectations could significantly influence how followers perceive social
presence and telepresence during live streaming sessions.

From a theoretical standpoint, this omission limits the ability to fully extend the
construct of social presence beyond its current cognitive -interactive definitions.
Specifically, it constrains understanding of how cultural psychology and sensory
design elements shape presence and, in turn, dynamic brand experiences.

5.4.1.2 Future Research
To address the limitations identified above, Future research will need to refine the
conceptual boundaries of presence by integrating aesthetic, cultural, and behavioural
dimensions into its theorisation.

For instance, investigating the effects of colour palettes, streamer presentation styles,
and interactive tools (e.g., live polls, personalised shoutouts, or audience -driven
content) could provide valuable insights into how to optimise social presence and
telepresence. Studying these factors would not only generate practical
recommendations but also extend existing presence theory by incorporating aesthetic
and design-related mechanisms into its conceptualisation.

In addition, given the significant cultural differences between countries, future research
should explore how cultural preferences in visual aesthetics and communication styles
influence followers’ perceptions of brand experiences. For example, Western
consumers may value a more casual and personable approach to live streaming, while

276
Asian audiences, particularly in China, may prefer a highly polished and professional
style. Investigating these cultural differences would refine the cross -cultural
boundaries of presence theory, showing whether its effects are universal or moderated
by cultural psychology factors such as individualism–collectivism and communication
norms.

Finally, future studies could employ experimental or longitudinal designs to test how
varying combinations of visual stimuli, interactive elements, and streamer engagement
tactics shape the sense of presence over time. Such approaches would advance
theory by clarifying the temporal and causal mechanisms through which presence
influences brand experience and follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour.
By controlling for both demographic and cultural variables, researchers could develop
more comprehensive models of presence that capture its cognitive, emotional, and
cultural dimensions, thereby strengthening both theoretical and practical knowledge in
the field of live streaming.

5.4.2  Construct Dimensionality
5.4.2.1 Limitation
This research develops a model to investigate the relationship between congruence
and dynamic brand experience, ultimately assessing their influence on follower
engagement and impulse purchasing behaviour. By highlighting the mediating role of
congruence between streamer and follower and dynamic brand experience, this study
advances the understanding of how congruence contributes to follower engagement
and impulse buying  behaviour in live streaming commerce. While the findings are

277
significant, several limitations should be acknowledged to clarify the boundaries of the
construct and open avenues for future theoretical development.

Although this research focuses on brand, streamer, and follower congruence, existing
literature suggests that congruence encompasses additional dimensions, such as
cultural congruence and product congruence, which were not addressed here. These
omitted dimensions may hold distinct and impactful roles in shaping dynamic brand
experiences. For example, cultural congruence refers to the alignment of cultural
values and norms between a brand’s messaging, the streamer’s persona, and
followers’ expectations. Cultural alignment may strengthen perceived authenticity and
evoke deeper connections, while cultural mismatches could trigger disinterest or even
negative brand evaluations. Likewise, the phenomenon of cultural collisions, where
diverse cultural elements inte ract, may create unique dynamics that heighten
engagement or stimulate curiosity (Ma, 2021; Lee, Yuan & Bi, 2023; Li, Xuan & Chen,
2024). Whether such collisions between Western brand identities and Chinese
consumer expectations amplify or diminish brand experiences remains unexplored,
leaving a gap in understanding how congruence operates across cultural boundaries.

Similarly, product congruence —defined as the alignment between different product
lines or series within the same brand —may also shape consumer perceptions and
engagement (Chen, Shen & Wei, 2023; Yu et al., 2024; Wang, 2024). For instance, a
streamer promoting high-end luxury items under a brand may reinforce perceptions of
exclusivity and quality, whereas promoting entry -level items under the same brand
could dilute that perception. This research does not examine how such internal brand

278
consistency (or inconsistency) interacts with follower expectations, limiting insights
into how product-level congruence contributes to dynamic brand experiences.

Finally, although the study controlled for key demographic variables such as age,
gender, education, and employment status, it did not account for external contextual
factors, including platform affordances, streamer –follower communication styles, or
market-specific trends. These contextual factors may interact with congruence in ways
that reshape its meaning or influence, suggesting that the current conceptualisation
may be under-specified.

5.4.2.2 Future Research
Future research should empirically investigate the additional dimensions of cultural
congruence and product congruence to refine the theoretical boundaries of the
congruence construct. For instance, examining how cultural alignment or
misalignment affects followers’ perceptions across different regions —particularly
contrasting Western and Asian markets, where cultural values and communication
styles diverge—would extend the construct by demonstrating its cultural contingency
(Ma, 2021; Lee, Yuan & Bi, 2023;  Li, Xuan & Chen, 2024). Integrating cultural
congruence into future models would not only enrich the understanding of consumer–
brand–streamer alignment but also clarify how cultural norms moderate or amplify the
role of congruence in shaping dynamic brand experiences. In this way, research could
contribute to cross -cultural consumer psychology and refine congruence theory by
embedding it in intercultural marketing contexts.

279
Similarly, future studies should examine the difference of product congruence,
determining whether consistent branding across product lines strengthens dynamic
brand experiences or whether variations across categories create differentiated
effects on engagement and impulse purchasing (Chen, Shen & Wei, 2023; Yu  et al.,
2024; Wang, 2024). This would extend the construct by unpacking its internal
structure—showing whether congruence functions at a single global level or as
multiple nested dimensions (e.g., cultural, product, and interpersonal congruence).
Experimental designs could test whether certain product types or brand tiers evoke
stronger or weaker responses, thereby refining the scope and applicability of
congruence theory in live streaming commerce.

Moreover, incorporating market -specific trends and platform affordances into future
research could reveal how contextual factors interact with congruence to shape
consumer perceptions. For example, followers on platforms with richer interactive
features (e.g., Douyin, Taobao Live) may perceive congruence differently than those
on less interactive platforms (e.g., TikTok UK). This would extend congruence theory
by situating it more firmly within platform -based and market-based contexts, offering
a more contextualised and flexible conceptualisation of the construct.

Collectively, these future directions highlight that congruence is not a static or one -
dimensional construct but rather a multifaceted theoretical concept shaped by cultural,
product, and contextual conditions. By exploring these dimensions, future researc h
can extend congruence theory, refine its conceptual boundaries, and strengthen its
explanatory power in predicting consumer engagement and impulse buying in live
streaming commerce.

280
5.4.3   Methodological Constraints
5.4.3.1 Limitation
This research employs a quantitative research approach to examine the mediating
effects of the sense of presence and congruence on the relationship between
streamers' attributes and dynamic brand experiences, alongside their influence on
follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour. While the findings significantly
contribute to the understanding of live streaming commerce, several limitations in the
research design and methodology warrant attention, providing opportunities for further
exploration.

First, a major limitation is the geographical focus of the research, which includes
participants from the UK and China only. This focus limits the generalisability of the
findings to other regions, particularly those with established live streaming markets
and unique cultural dynamics. For instance, countries like the United States, Brazil,
and Indonesia are known for their substantial online live streaming audiences
(Statista,2024), but consumer behaviour in these contexts remains unexplored in this
research. Such as cultural factors, which significantly influence consumer -brand
interactions, may yield different results in these regions (Li et al., 2024; Peng, Zhang
& Huang, 2024; Wang et al., 2024) . For example, cultural preferences regarding
communication styles, interaction levels, and digital consumption behaviours might
impact the mediating effects of the sense of presence and congruence differently,
offering opportunities for more globally incl usive research. Without testing the model
in these diverse contexts, the theoretical claims regarding cross -cultural robustness
remain partial.

281
Second, t he reliance on a questionnaire -based methodology introduces additional
limitations. While questionnaires effectively capture self -reported behaviours and
perceptions, they are prone to biases such as social desirability bias and recall bias,
which may compromise the accuracy of the data (Bryman, 2016). These limitations
may restrict the explanatory depth of theories applied in this study, particularly when
exploring different psychological constructs like social presence, congruence, and
impulse buying. Without triangulation from behavioural data (e.g., actual purchase
logs, platform analytics) or qualitative insights (e.g., interviews, digital ethnography),
theoretical interpretations may remain somewhat abstract and disconnected from lived
consumer experiences.

Third, the cross -sectional design of this study limits its ability to capture how the
relationships among streamers’ attributes, presence, congruence, and dynamic brand
experiences evolve over time. Live streaming commerce is inherently dynamic, with
consumers’ responses influenced by repeated exposure, novelty effects, or even
fatigue. The absence of longitudinal data means the study cannot theories how the
constructs studied—particularly presence and congruence—may fluctuate across time
or different stages of consumer -brand relationships. This weakens the potential to
contribute to temporal theories of follower engagement and impulse buying behaviour
in digital environments.

5.4.3.2 Future Research
Future studies should consider incorporating qualitative methods, such as in -depth
interviews to complement quantitative approaches. While this study provides statistical
validation of the conceptual model, qualitative methods could uncover the

282
psychological mechanisms (e.g., emotional immersion, cognitive absorption) through
which presence and congruence shape consumer responses. Such mixed -method
approaches would not only enrich practical insights but also extend theoretical
frameworks like the  SOR model by capturing different, experiential data that
quantitative surveys may miss.

Longitudinal research designs would also address the limitations of cross -sectional
studies by examining how follower engagement and impulse buying  behaviour and
perceptions change over time (Bell, 2019). For instance, longitudinal studies could test
whether the effects of presence and congruence intensify with repeated exposure or
diminish due to novelty loss and fatigue. This would allow researchers to theorise
presence and congruence as temporally dyna mic constructs, refining existing digital
marketing theories that currently treat them as static effects.

Expanding the geographical scope in future research to include a more diverse range
of countries would enhance the generalisability of the findings and provide richer
insights into cross -cultural differences. Examining regions such as Southeast Asia,
North America, and South America (Statista,2024) could reveal how cultural
orientations—such as collectivism versus individualism, or high versus low power
distance—moderate the effects of presence and congruence. Such cross -cultural
comparisons would advance cultural psychology and international marketing theories
by clarifying the boundary conditions under which the proposed framework operates.

Industry-specific research could also prove valuable. For instance, testing this model
within sectors like tourism (Zhang, Wang & Zhang, 2021; Liu et al., 2024) , education

283
(Hou et al., 2023) , and food (Guo, Zhang & Wang, 2022; Liu et al., 2024; Nguyen &
Veer, 2024) could refine the scope of congruence theory by exploring whether different
product categories amplify or dampen the role of presence. This would extend theory
by demonstrating how industry context functions as a boundary condition for consumer
engagement in live streaming commerce.

Finally, future research could explore additional mediators and moderators, such as
platform-specific features (Li, Xuan & Chen, 2024; Long, Zaidin & Mai, 2024; Zhang
et al., 2024) . For example, features like real -time gifting systems on Douyin or live
polls on TikTok may amplify social presence. Also streamer interaction styles (Cong
& Li, 2024), or consumer personality traits (Lin et al., 2021; Zheng et al., 2023). Such
variables would enrich theoretical models by explaining why presence and congruence
vary in their effectiveness across different technological environments and consumer
segments. In doing so, future work could extend existing models of follower
engagement and impulse buying  behaviour by incorporating technology affordances
and individual difference variables into frameworks of live streaming commerce.

In summary, by addressing these methodological limitations, future studies could
refine theoretical boundaries (e.g., presence as dynamic, congruence as
multidimensional, cultural values as moderators) while also building a more robust and
globally applicable framework. This dual contribution would not only expand academic
knowledge but also provide actionable insights for marketers and streamers in the
increasingly competitive field of digital marketing.

284
5.4.4 Conclusion
This dissertation set out to quantitative investigate how streamers’ attributes, sense of
presence, and brand –streamer–follower congruence shape dynamic brand
experiences and, in turn, influence follower engagement and impulse buying in live
streaming commerce. By integrating these constructs with in a unified framework and
testing the model across two culturally distinct contexts —the United Kingdom and
China—the study provides new insights into the mechanisms driving follower
engagement and impulse buying  behaviour in this rapidly expanding digital
environment.

The findings confirm that streamers’ attributes exert a significant influence on follower
engagement and impulse buying behaviour, but primarily through the mediating effects
of presence and congruence. Dynamic brand experience emerged as a central
pathway, linking these mediators to follower engagement and impulse buying. Notably,
the results reveal important cultural distinctions:  while presence strongly enhanced
brand experience in China, its role was less pronounced in the UK, suggesting that
platform design, digital familiarity, and cultural expectations shape how immersive
cues are perceived.

The research makes several theoretical contributions. It extends congruence theory
and source credibility theory by demonstrating their relevance in live streaming
commerce and by integrating sense of presence as a key psychological mechanism.
It also advances the literature on dynamic brand experience by situating it within a live,
interactive environment rather than static social media contexts. Together, these
contributions provide a richer conceptual understanding of how digital interactivity,

285
alignment, and immersion jointly drive consumer engagement and purchase
behaviour.

In practical terms, the study offers clear guidance for digital marketers and live
streamers. Success in live streaming commerce requires more than product
promotion: it depends on fostering immersive and interactive environments, ensuring
congruence betwe en streamers, brands, and followers, and tailoring strategies to
cultural contexts. These insights provide actionable strategies for enhancing
engagement, trust, and impulse buying across diverse markets.

Although the study provides empirical evidence, it is not without limitations. Its
geographical scope was restricted to the UK and China, and its reliance on cross -
sectional survey data precludes longitudinal insights. Future research should expand
to othe r regions, adopt mixed and longitudinal methods, and examine additional
moderators such as platform features, industry dynamics, and consumer traits.

In conclusion, this dissertation contributes to advancing theoretical and managerial
understanding of live streaming commerce by offering a comprehensive framework
that explains how presence, congruence, and dynamic brand experience mediate the
relationship between streamer attributes and follower engagement and impulse buying
behaviour. It demonstrates the universal power of immersive and congruent brand
experiences while highlighting important cultural differences. Ultimately, the research
deepens scholarly knowledge of digital marketing while providing practical strategies
for brands and streamers operating in one of the most dynamic frontiers of consumer
engagement.

286
